STAT+: Pharmalittle: We’re reading about an approval for novel drug for strokes, a warning for GLP-1 drugs, and more

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Ed’s stories explore prescription drug pricing, affordability and access, as well issues surrounding patents, litigation, and legislation. He is also the author of the morning Pharmalittle newsletter and the afternoon Pharmalot newsletter.

Top of the morning to you and a fine one it is. Blue skies, cool breezes, and plenty of chirping birds are enveloping the comfy Pharmalot campus, which is cause to fire up the coffee kettle for yet another cup of stimulation. Our choice today is pistachio. Meanwhile, we have assembled the latest menu of tidbits for you to peruse. We hope you have a wonderful day and please do keep in touch. Once again, we will note that our settings have changed to accept postcards and telegrams …

The Food and Drug Administration approved a Bayer drug to reduce recurrence of stroke, making available the first in a novel class of medications that hold the promise of preventing blood clots more safely, STAT notes. Many blood thinners are not used in this setting due to the increased risk of bleeding, but the drug, which will be sold under the brand name Sunharbo, uses a mechanism that is thought to stop the formation of bad clots without increasing the risk of bleeding. Experts say the data showed a significant advance in an area that has seen little innovation over the past few decades. Secondary strokes are estimated to affect about 20% of people who have had a first stroke and can cause severe disability or death.

The FDA warned that GLP-1 drugs carry a potential risk for a rare but serious eye condition and required pharmaceutical companies to inform patients and doctors of the risk, STAT writes. The condition, called non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy, or NAION, is sometimes referred to as an “eye stroke” and is caused by insufficient blood flow to the optic nerve. It typically causes sudden vision loss and, in severe cases, can lead to blindness. There are currently no proven treatments for the condition. The FDA said it started looking into this risk after researchers in 2024 published an observational study linking Ozempic and Wegovy to the eye condition.

Bitdeer reports record Q3 revenue as net loss widens to $125.5 million

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The miner's net loss widened to $125.5 million despite higher mining output and AI Cloud revenue lifting quarterly sales to a record $320.7 million.

Bitdeer reported record third-quarter revenue of $320.7 million, up 89% from a year earlier, as the bitcoin miner expanded its mining operations and increased AI cloud revenue.

The Singapore-based miner's net loss widened to $125.5 million from $30.1 million in the same period last year, the company said in a statement on Tuesday. The loss included a non-cash $54.6 million charge tied to changes in the fair value of a digital-asset loan.

Bitdeer CFO Michael G. Potter said the company's adjusted EBITDA, which excludes several non-cash and other items, rose 173% to $69.6 million from $25.5 million.

Bitdeer mined 3,855 BTC in the quarter, up from 1,109 BTC a year earlier, as its total proprietary hashrate climbed to 86.4 EH/s from 35 EH/s, the company said.

Self-mining revenue rose to $217.5 million from $130.9 million, while co-mining contributed another $59.9 million. The company held just 47 BTC as of Sept. 30, down from 2,029 BTC a year earlier.

Bitdeer also reported $221.5 million in proceeds from digital-asset disposals during the quarter, primarily mined bitcoin, according to the statement. It ended September with $43.2 million in digital assets and $343.8 million in digital-asset receivables from a related party, bringing the combined balance to $387 million.

Meanwhile, AI cloud revenue increased to $20.4 million from $1.8 million a year earlier. The company reported an AI cloud revenue backlog of approximately $2.9 billion and said it had secured about 332 IT megawatts of AI cloud capacity.

"We are converting AI demand into long-duration contracts," Potter said. "Our AI Cloud revenue backlog has grown to approximately $2.9 billion, and construction at Tydal remains on target. Customer prepayments, together with project-level financing, are designed to fund AI capacity as it is contracted."

Bitdeer shares closed 9.9% higher at $6.61 on Monday, according to The Block's BTDR price page. The stock was down 2.7% in premarket trading on Tuesday.

What Car Looks Best On Aftermarket Wheels?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Let's face it: Most stock wheels are, at best, fine. Occasionally one will stand out for its looks, usually because it's been made by Mercedes, but most OEM wheels are just some basic metal that you can wrap a tire around. That's good enough for getting from point A to point B, but we car enthusiasts often want better. Enter the entire world of aftermarket wheels, in any style you could dream up — rally turbofans, classic five-spokes, even teddy bears.

Today, though, we're not here to talk about the wheels themselves. We're here to talk about the cars they go on, and that brings us to a very broad question: What car looks best on aftermarket wheels? I'll accept answers to this in two forms, either cars that most need aftermarket wheels or cars that look best on the most different designs of aftermarket wheel. Are those criteria wide-open enough for you?

For both versions of the question, I think there's a single answer: The Toyobaru, most specifically the first-generation Scion FR-S/Subaru BRZ. It's a car that looks great on lightweight track wheels like Volk TE37s or Titan 7 T-S5s, but its style is just as well-suited to a set of bags and a BBS mesh design. Even a vintage wheel like the RS Watanabe, when scaled up to modern fitment, looks great on the Toyobaru chassis.

I think the car that looks best on aftermarket wheels is the first-generation Scion FR-S/Subaru BRZ, but what do you think? Do you think the Civic most needs a new set of shoes, or that the Lexus LS400 can do no wrong with any style of wheel face? Let me know in the comments below, and I'll collect my favorite answers later in the week. Bonus points if you've tried aftermarket wheels on a chassis yourself.

Lucid says service wait times fell 20% after 40% jump in service staff

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Lucid (LCID) says service wait times dropped 20% globally in the third quarter after it grew its technician and service-support staff by more than 40% in a single quarter.

The automaker is also adding Studio and Service Centers in Southern California, Los Angeles, Dallas, and the Chicago suburbs, plus 20 more Gravity Mobile Service vans. It’s a clear sign that service had become a problem for Lucid owners.

Lucid announced the numbers today, a day after its Q3 2026 earnings. Both comparisons are against the second quarter, not a year ago.

The hiring isn’t done either. After a 40%-plus increase in technicians, advisors, and concierges in Q3, Lucid wants that figure at 50% by the end of 2026 and 60% by Q1 2027. Mobile Service capacity in North America grew 15%, with a 40% target for year-end.

That’s a big hiring push for a company that laid off 18% of its workforce in June, its second cut in four months after a 12% reduction in February. Roughly 1,500 people left across those two rounds.

And owners noticed. On the LucidOwners forum, one Air Touring owner posted in early October that Lucid told them in writing to expect “a service time of about 7 weeks” at its Beverly Hills Service Center. Other threads blamed service-department layoffs for slow repairs and recall work.

Billy Hayes, Lucid’s Chief Customer Officer, said the company is “adding service personnel, and increasing Mobile Service capacity to reduce wait times and provide more convenient, responsive support.”

Here’s what’s opening, according to Lucid:

Lucid is also letting a growing number of its Certified Collision shops handle recall campaign work, which should take some pressure off its own Service Centers.

Outside the US, the expansion leans on partners. Munsterhuis Autobedrijven is Lucid’s first retail partner in the Netherlands (Hengelo), Cloppenburg Automobil SE opens a Duisburg retail and service site in January 2027, Autohaus Possögel starts authorized service in Naumburg, Germany, in December, and Emil Frey France will support Lucid’s launch in France. In Saudi Arabia, Lucid opened its first company-owned Body & Paint Repair Center in Riyadh, with more service sites in Riyadh and Dammam coming in the first half of 2027.

Lucid doesn’t say what the average wait time actually is.

A 20% drop from seven weeks is still more than five weeks. A 20% drop from one week is a non-issue. Without a baseline, owners can’t tell which one they’re getting.

It’s also hard to separate better service from fewer cars coming in. Lucid delivered 3,806 vehicles in Q3, down 7% year-over-year, while it worked through inventory. Its fleet keeps growing, but slowly.

For context, Rivian delivered 19,248 vehicles in Q3, five times Lucid’s volume, and it’s been leaning hard on mobile service too. Lucid is a much smaller operation trying to cover the US, Europe, and the Middle East with a thin network.

This is the right move, and it’s overdue.

Lucid has been one of the few EV startups with genuinely class-leading hardware. The Air’s efficiency is still unmatched and the Gravity is a great vehicle on paper, even if we found its software wasn’t ready. But when you’re asking someone to spend $80,000 or more on a car from a brand with an uncertain future, the ownership experience is half the sale. A seven-week wait for a repair kills that sale, and the referrals that come after it.

So growing service staff by 40% in a quarter is a big deal. It also tells you how thin those teams had gotten after two rounds of layoffs. Lucid cut too deep in the wrong place, and it’s now hiring to fix it.

The problem is the timing and the money. Lucid burned $1.04 billion in free cash flow in Q3 and only stays funded into late 2027 because the PIF just added another $1.75 billion. Meanwhile, the Midsize “Cosmos” that’s supposed to bring real volume doesn’t start production until the second half of 2027. Service is exactly where Lucid should spend between now and then, because the buyers it has today are the ones who’ll decide whether anybody trusts the brand with a cheaper EV.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. If You Didn’t Already Think CrossFit Was a Cult, This Book Will Convince You

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

CrossFit could easily hold the distinction of being the most popular—and certainly the most universally recognized—fitness movement ever. At its peak, the company, founded in 2000, boasted over 15,000 affiliate gyms, or “boxes,” in more than 150 countries and was enlisting nearly 50,000 coaches each year. Somewhere along the line, to many people (including my mom), the word CrossFit became synonymous for working out.

But you may have noticed that people don’t talk as much about CrossFit nowadays as they used to. Following an assorted variety of allegations against its mercurial founder Greg Glassman in 2020, and the tragic and seemingly avoidable death of an athlete at the 2024 CrossFit Games, the company has been gradually losing its chalky grip on popular culture.

But the storytelling around CrossFit’s rise and fall is anything but done and dusted, as I learned while reading Calum Marsh’s new book, The Glass Box—a fascinating, staggeringly detailed account of not only CrossFit’s rocket-propelled rise and spectacular flame-out, as told by insider sources including Glassman himself, but a trove of previously unshared anecdotes that make it hard to put down.

A few days after the book’s late-October release, I spoke to Marsh about airing CrossFit’s dirtiest laundry, how the cultishness of CrossFit has spread to other fitness crazes, and why an old-fashioned barbell snatch is the ultimate kryptonite for the modern performative male.

Calum Marsh: The first 50 people I spoke to were basically warning me off the subject, telling me that it was going to be an uphill battle and that if the people that I was writing about were unhappy with how I wrote about them, they would criticize me or attempt to defame me. They have a long history, which I've chronicled in the book, of creating campaigns of bad will towards their critics. They are really experienced at mobilizing the internet and mobilizing social media in order to discredit people that they consider critics or, as I sometimes frame it in the book, heretics.

But, on the other hand, I knew that I was going to be approaching this from a journalistically rigorous perspective, and that it was going to be an account of what actually happened, from the people who were involved. It wasn't going to be a book about my opinion or my personal take on CrossFit. So I felt quite secure that the things I was writing in the book were the best possible representation of the truth.

There's been a lot of focus on some of the more, shall we say, sordid details. There was an article in the New York Post that basically pulled all that out and made the book seem like it was some scandalous hit piece. That's not the book I tried to write, and it's not the book I think I wrote. The book I wrote is a very fair, even-handed assessment of a man and the business that he created. It includes true stories about his behavior that may not be the most flattering, and it includes, I think, testament to the brilliance of what he created and all of the great things that he did.

It's been amusing to see the reception somewhat split among some hardcore CrossFitters. They're upset because they think it's a takedown of Greg Glassman that's biased against him. And then other critics think that I'm way too generous to him, or way too flattering. Someone said I was too affectionate towards him. It's funny to me that two different people can read it and have polar opposite views. But hopefully that means that it's just objective.

It’s something that is always mentioned in relation to CrossFit. There’s actually another book that came out while I was writing my book, called The Cult of CrossFit by Katie Rose Hejtmanek. It's an academic study of the history and sociology of cults, and how CrossFit is a cult by definition. I thought that was really illuminating. There are a lot of similarities that go beyond all the joking, and there are a lot of things about the cult-like nature of CrossFit that have made it intoxicating for some people, and maybe painful in other ways as well.

The thing that struck me was how a lot of people, especially earlier CrossFit enthusiasts or devotees, talked to me about the difficulty they had leaving CrossFit. Greg has said many times, and will still say, that they weren't a cult because you could leave at any time. And I always found that to be a fairly unconvincing argument, because many cults say the same thing. A lot of CrossFitters had this experience where they felt like they had really close friends—sometimes even family members or partners—who essentially excommunicated them—cut them off, iced them out, however you want to phrase it—after they either left CrossFit or spoke out against CrossFit. That, to me, is the strongest cult-like parallel.

It really wasn't. I had heard from people that Glassman was generous, and so I knew stories about him spending lavishly on dinners, for example. But it all seemed pretty much within the realm of normal spending for someone who's massively wealthy. It wasn't until I started speaking to people who were more familiar with CrossFit’s finances when I started to learn about the degree to which the spending was out of control. The amount of money they spent relative to what they earned is the most shocking thing to me, because it's not like they were spending that much money while wildly out-earning it. They were almost spending more than they were making. And they were not slowing their spending when the earning slowed. That, to me, as a business reporter, is very, very interesting, because you start to wonder how they let that happen. Was no one there to say, ‘Hey, we need to pump the brakes a little bit?’ As it turned out, there really wasn't.

A lot of people had this reckoning in 2020 after [Glassman’s George Floyd tweet] and some of the accusations that came out about Glassman then. A lot of gyms deaffiliated from CrossFit, and Greg, of course, stepped down and sold the company. I personally know many people who had a kind of personal reckoning with their level of comfort in supporting the company. But I think that was, at least in some cases, answered by Greg's departure.

Others went through this process of divorcing themselves from the CrossFit identity as a brand, but continuing to practice CrossFit as a methodology. There are thousands of gyms all over the world that are CrossFit gyms in all but name. They probably call themselves cross-training or functional fitness now, but if you go in, they still have a Rogue rig, they still have wall balls, they still do snatches—everything you'd expect to see if you walked into a CrossFit gym. The people there are still CrossFitters at heart. They've still given themselves over to the transformative power of the methodology that Greg Glassman invented. But they are no longer endorsing or supporting the brand or the company at large.

I would be willing to bet that if you walked into any CrossFit gym in North America and asked them what the difference is between when Glassman was in charge and when corporate private equity took charge, they wouldn’t know that even happened. The average person, who just goes to CrossFit to get fit and doesn't follow the CrossFit news and isn't obsessed with CrossFit Games, would not have any idea. And this is partly a consequence of the fact that the affiliate model is so loose.

I first started going to a box in 2018. I was doing personal training with someone at what I didn't realize was even a CrossFit box. I was doing that personal training for probably about a year before I realized I was doing CrossFit, and then I started doing classes in 2019.

I do! In fact, I coached a CrossFit class earlier this morning.

Yeah. Well, they don't allow you to call it a certification with the one I have. They're very clear about that language. But I do have my CrossFit Level 1 certificate. I did the course and passed the test.

I think that part of it is just the nature of fandom. If you become enthusiastic about something, you want to identify as an enthusiast, and that means wearing the T-shirt. I went to a Bob Dylan concert on the weekend. I bought a Bob Dylan hat. It's just part of the experience of being a fan of something. I think Hyrox is super interesting, because it does have a lot of cult-like similarities with CrossFit. But the really toxic side—the side that's a bit more nefarious, that involves really giving your life over to it—I haven't seen with Hyrox, at least yet. I feel like there's something missing at the center, possibly the guru figure that Greg Glassman was for CrossFit. Hyrox doesn't really have that. So maybe that missing ingredient has stopped it from being as intense. But, on the whole, I think it's just this level of fandom that is baked into everything in culture now.

There's a journalist named Hannah Singleton, who writes about fitness a lot. She recently wrote a piece about how athleticism is the new flex for people, and I think that’s sort of the direction we're moving in. For example, Hyrox. It's in the same vein as running a marathon or doing an ultra race, where it's an achievement that you can brandish. You can put it on your LinkedIn. You can say that you've done this and that. Maybe it's on your resume. You're almost putting up points on your scoreboard. And I think that we're probably going to find either more events along those lines, where it's something that you can be proud of because it's so difficult, or a system in which you can have an overall fitness score. I think people really want to show that they put in the work. They want to impress people with how fit they are. In the same way that people who are into running are obsessively posting about their marathon time or their 10K time, and those milestones are universally understood in running—everyone knows how impressive it is to run a sub-10-minute 5K or a sub-three-hour marathon—the fitness equivalent of that, I think, is still coming.

This is one of the things I find interesting in light of CrossFit—and I know that some people are going to think this is not true—but I really do think CrossFit, or let’s say functional fitness, is an antidote to this whole performative thing. It's so hard that it's humbling. You can’t really show up and pretend you did it. You cannot performatively do barbell snatches. If you see someone on social media snatching heavy, it's because they've worked at it for hundreds, if not thousands, of hours, and because they're really strong, really mobile, and really fit. And I say this as someone who both has a copy of Infinite Jest on my bookshelf and wears Satisfy.

You can ‘perform’ your sport of choice by posting about it, but things like snatches or ring muscle ups are so physically demanding that it kind of cuts through that bullshit. And I think that we're probably going to increasingly value things that we know on-site are authentic, because there's an authenticity built into it. There's no shortcut there. And that authenticity will become more and more valuable.

Via: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Over 95% of Via's code is now written with AI, a productivity gain that may matter more to margins than to new contracts.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 9, 2026, Via (VIA +12.80%) announced revenue grew 31% year over year to $144 million as customer contracts expanded. Broadening software adoption across municipal transit agencies and disciplined spending are moving the transit technology provider toward net profitability. Three takeaways from management's comments illustrate how the business is evolving.

When Via manages an entire transit network, it replaces pen-and-paper systems with software across fleet scheduling, maintenance, and dispatching. These end-to-end contracts yield immediate efficiency gains for transit agencies, so Via doesn't have to rebuild its sales effort for every new city.

Rising fuel prices and vehicle ownership costs are pushing commuters toward public transportation. U.S. voters recently approved ballot initiatives that will generate $30 billion in local funding. That unlocks fresh expansion opportunities for Via's transit software.

Automated engineering and customer support tools are lowering internal operating expenses across the business. Software automation helped raise adjusted gross margin to 40.5%. The company doesn't need to bloat headcount to ship new features faster.

Revenue expanded 31% year over year in the third quarter while operating expenses grew at a much slower rate. Regional customer density creates flywheel effects that lower sales costs in neighboring cities, while internal software automation accelerates product delivery. Management is tracking sales pipeline conversion and gross margin consistency.

Expanding into school transportation extends Via's core platform into adjacent municipal markets. Converting a record pipeline while locking in profitability defines the primary operational focus heading into next year.

Apples new iOS 27.2.1 fixes critical bugs, and you should install it now

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Apple's new iOS 27.2.1, released on Monday, doesn't bring any new features, but that doesn't mean you should sleep on it.

In fact, you should install it right away, as it includes patches for numerous bugs, including some critical software vulnerabilities.

Apple's official release notes for this release (alongside iPadOS, which was also updated to version 27.2.1) highlight nine separate bugs, including one in which an app could be used to access sensitive user data, one in which an attacker could pair with a user's device without user interaction, and one in which a remote attacker could be able to join an iMessage group chat and receive its messages.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

One particularly nasty bug was highlighted on Tuesday by cryptocurrency exchange Binance, which infamously had a massive security breach in late October.

"A vulnerability (worse_query) affecting iOS 26.7.1, 27.0.1, and 27.2 allows malicious apps to read local files from other apps, putting seed phrases, private keys, and login credentials at risk across the entire ecosystem. This is an iOS-level issue, not unique to Binance," wrote Binance in a tweet, urging users to delete apps installed from untrusted sources, and to update iOS and all apps to the latest official versions.

Apple's iOS 27.2.1 is available to iOS users through Settings -> General -> Software Update. Users who haven't switched to iOS 27 should install the latest version of their OS; namely, iOS 26.7.3 or iOS 18.7.12.

‘Purple Rain’ Musical: Jordan Fisher Is New Prince Of Broadway

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Broadway‘s Purple Rain has cast Jordan Fisher in the lead role of The Kid, the very Prince-like character portrayed by very real Prince in the original 1984 film.

Producer Orin Wolf announced the casting today. Purple Rain will premiere next spring at the Majestic Theatre with previews beginning Friday, March 12, 2027, and the official opening on Monday, April 12, 2027.

Fisher’s many Broadway credits include Christian in Moulin Rouge!, Anthony in Sweeney Todd: The Demon Barber of Fleet Street, Evan in Dear Evan Hansen, and Laurens/Philip in Hamilton. Most recently, her starred as Seymour in the Off-Broadway revival of Little Shop of Horrors. Related Stories News 'Here There Are Blueberries' Broadway Review: Evil As Banal As A Bowl Of Fruit News 'Wanted' Broadway Review: Historical Musical Plays With Gospel Truth

Purple Rain‘s The Kid will mark the first time Jordan has created a role in a new Broadway musical. Watch on Deadline

Fisher is known to TV viewers for his roles in Netflix’s Hello, Goodbye, and Everything in Between. His credits also include To All the Boys: P.S. I Still Love You, Work It, Rent: Live!, Grease: Live!, and Disney/Pixar’s Turning Red. He is the Founder and CEO of Jordan Fisher Productions, a production company focused on curating collaborative theater and film projects that spark meaningful conversations.

“We know that Jordan Fisher is an extraordinary talent,” said director Saheem Ali, “but what’s been thrilling about watching him inhabit ‘The Kid’ is discovering the fire beneath the surface: the swagger, vulnerability and restless creative spirit we recognize in Prince. Jordan brings all of that to the role, along with an infectious generosity that makes everyone around him better.”

Ali added, “He’s finding something raw, surprising, and deeply human in this iconic character, and I can’t wait for Broadway audiences to experience it.”

Additional casting will be announced at a later date.

More than 20 Prince hits, including “When Doves Cry,” “I Would Die 4 U,” “Take Me with You,” and “The Beautiful Ones,” are included in the Purple Rain stage adaptation, which features music by Prince, direction by Tony-nominated director Saheem Ali (Buena Vista Social Club), a book by playwright and screenwriter Peter Duchan, a music team led by Jason Michael Webb, and choreography by Ebony Williams.

Bobby Z and Morris Hayes, both of whom worked with Prince for years as bandmates and musical collaborators, serve the production as Music Advisers.

The synopsis: “Minneapolis, 1984. A new era of music television is creating overnight stars. A magnetic young performer known as The Kid seems like the perfect frontman to lead his bandmates to stardom. But when his past threatens everything he’s built, he’ll have to sing his way out of the darkness and into the spotlight where he belongs.”

The film told the fictional story of The Kid, an up-and-coming rock musician in the Minneapolis club scene, as he contended with a tumultuous home environment, a rival band, and a budding romance. Released in July 1984 by Warner Bros, the film was originated by Prince, directed by Albert Magnoli and written by Magnoli and William Blinn. It was Prince’s film debut and grossed close to $100 million worldwide, winning the Academy Award for Best Original Score.

Another report backs OLED iPad Air early next year

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Following the recent update to the iPad mini, we’re expecting the iPad Air to be next in line to be upgraded to an OLED screen.

Previous reports have gradually narrowed down the launch to early 2027, and a new supply chain report echoes this …

OLED screens were initially exclusive to the iPad Pro. That finally changed last month when Apple gave the iPad mini its biggest update in years. The new model is thinner and lighter and powered by an A20 Pro chip, while the headline news is the new OLED screen.

The iPad Air got a chip update earlier this year, but nothing else. An upgrade to OLED is expected next year, with recent reports suggesting that it will be in the first half of 2027. That’s now backed by a new supply chain report by DigiTimes.

Given past timings, the smart money would be on the OLED iPad Air being announced in March. With the precedent set by the latest iPad mini, we can likely expect the new iPad Air to have similar specs, which means sticking with Touch ID and a 60Hz display.

Polestar has a new look with its Formula 2030 concept

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The shape looks like a Saab Aero-X, but the nose previews the next Polestar 2.

Perhaps only a small percentage of Ars readers will care about the Polestar 2030 concept, debuted today by the electric automaker. Spun out of Volvo—once upon a time, Polestar was a racing team—the brand has leveraged the resources of parent-automaker Geely to create electric vehicles that marry a modern minimalist style with rewarding driving dynamics. But you now can’t buy any Polestars in the US, since Geely is Chinese, and banning Chinese-owned car brands enjoys remarkable bipartisan support in Congress.

The Formula 2030 is about the same length as a Polestar 2 but with a very low roofline. If anything, the two-box shape reminds me of the Saab Aero-X, one of the final concepts from the then-terminal Swedish automaker. (In many ways, Polestar has adopted Saab’s mantle.)

Polestar says the concept fits into its own segment somewhere between the next Polestar 2 and the Polestar 7—it numbers its cars chronologically (mostly), not based on size—although it’s not saying if the hatchback form factor will go into series production. Successfully packaging the rear seat passengers and a lithium-ion battery pack is something a concept needn’t worry about, after all. Rather, it’s a styling exercise, or more than one.

The nose is a good preview of the next Polestar 2, and the rear light signature will show up on production Polestars. “We wanted to evolve, while respecting, the core design values we demonstrated with previous models that define the Polestar brand in such a recognizable way,” said Nahum Escobedo, head of exterior design at Polestar.

“And with Formula 2030, with its clear Polestar design attributes, like the Dual Blade headlights, wraparound visor-like windshield, and powerful haunches, we have done just that. But we’ve pushed perceptions, preconceptions, and the expected as well, with our considered choices in form language, amplification of the performance topic, and new detailing, like the Single Blade rear lights,” Escobedo said.

Polestar says the four-seat interior pushes its usual minimalism even further. But the end result has avoided the bland fate suffered by the recent Range Rover GT concept, for example. Even though Polestar is committed to developing autonomous driving technology, for manual modes it has added physical controls—rocker switches and rotary controllers, with a user-configurable UI. That’s a new thing for Polestar.

You can’t really tell from the photos, but the Formula 2030 concept explores some sustainability ideas as well. Like BMW’s I Vision Circular, the Formula 2030 was designed using monomaterials and with recycling in mind where possible. The interiors of the doors are fully recyclable, there’s low-emission steel in the chassis, and biocomposites in place of traditional plastics.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Disney and Adobe are building custom generative AI models for parks and ESPN

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Adobe will give Disney early access to Firefly, Premiere and Photoshop under a multi-year alliance announced Monday

Walt Disney $DIS and Adobe $ADBE are building custom generative AI models for Disney’s theme parks, studios and ESPN under a multi-year alliance the companies announced on Monday at Adobe MAX in Miami Beach, Florida.

As an Official AI Innovation Partner of Disney, Adobe will provide early access to its AI, creative and media technologies, the company said. Those include Adobe Firefly, Adobe Firefly Foundry, Adobe Premiere and Adobe Photoshop, along with dedicated engineering and research resources for Disney's production pipeline.

Through Firefly Foundry, Disney will collaborate with Adobe on proprietary generative AI systems built around its own franchises and properties, Adobe said. The models learn from character attributes, environments and visual styles, which lets them produce output that is safe for commercial use and ready for production.

Walt Disney Imagineering Research & Development has used Firefly Foundry in theme park development, Adobe said. Its tools include a sketch-to-image model that takes artists' hand-drawn ideas and renders them as 2D concept art, plus a custom image model that produces assets faithful to the franchises.

The work will begin by shortening project schedules in Disney's Experiences division and supporting early concept work at StudioLAB, Walt Disney Studios' internal innovation arm, according to Reuters.

"We talk about the time frames in our projects in years — it's years' worth of development. And what Adobe Foundry has allowed us to do is compress the creative process that would have taken months to now being able to do that in hours and days," Kyle Laughlin, Disney's senior vice president of R&D technology and engineering, told Reuters.

Laughlin said the tools will help the team avoid expensive modifications on site after construction of park experiences gets under way. He said they are "in no way meant to replace" roles at Imagineering, according to Reuters.

Adobe joins StudioLAB as an Innovation Partner to explore internal creative workflows with Disney creatives and technologists, according to Adobe. At ESPN Edge, the sports network's innovation center, ESPN and Adobe will identify ways for AI to increase engagement with sports fans and to bring ESPN's archive to life.

The alliance also covers marketing activations, industry events and customer storytelling tied to Walt Disney World Resort, Disneyland Resort, Disney Cruise Line, ESPN Edge and StudioLAB. Adobe will increase its presence across Disney's media platforms. Co-innovation projects with StudioLAB and ESPN Edge are under way, and more activations will roll out over the alliance's term, Adobe said.

"Today's announcement marks the next chapter of the relationship," Hannah Elsakr, Adobe's vice president of GenAI New Business Ventures, said in a statement.

Becca Vodnoy, Disney's senior vice president of Corporate Alliances, said in a statement that the alliance gives Disney's creative teams "new ways to explore and develop their ideas."

To streamline Hellberg’s squad and make way for new signings, we put forward three players who Middlesbrough should consider offloading in the winter…

Experienced centre-back George Edmundson was lined up for a move away from Middlesbrough late in the summer transfer window, but a deal never materialised. He has not been named in a Championship matchday squad since the opening-day win over Lincoln City and is firmly down the pecking order under Hellberg.

The powerful centre-back could be a shrewd signing for a team towards the bottom end of the Championship, or in the top six of League One.

Jensen Huang's affair aided at least $7.5 billion in AI chip shipments to China, report says

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Nvidia's board has launched an internal investigation, and the FBI is examining whether the CEO was targeted by foreign intelligence

Nvidia $NVDA CEO Jensen Huang’s relationship with a Chinese businesswoman suspected of spying for Beijing helped enable at least $7.5 billion in Nvidia chip shipments to Chinese companies, according to The Information, which cited two people who know her. Reuters said it could not independently verify the claims.

The businesswoman, Alice Huang, assembled a web of firms spanning China and Singapore, partnering with her sister and husband to obtain the chips, according to The Information. She is not related to Jensen Huang and is also known as Huang Le. When orders for chips caught up in U.S. export curbs stalled, she leaned on her connections at Nvidia and its distributors to lock in supply, according to The Information. That effort got roughly 240,000 chips, spanning H100, H200 and some A100 models, into China.

Shanghai Century Litong, a company based in China and founded in June 2023, sat at the hub of the network, according to The Information. By October 2023, it had been designated a preferred cloud partner by Nvidia, which put it first in line for the chips. Century Litong leased out computing capacity and ran data centers built on Nvidia chips, some of which are prohibited from being sold to China. A portion of the hardware ended up supporting Alibaba, Tencent and several smaller AI labs in China, according to The Information.

Alice Huang held a senior role at Megaspeed, a Singapore-registered company that U.S. authorities have investigated over the flow of Nvidia chips from Southeast Asia into China, according to The Information. Megaspeed denies wrongdoing and says it abides by all relevant regulations, according to Bloomberg. Nvidia has said before that its own review of Megaspeed turned up no sign of diversion.

Jensen Huang confirmed the affair on Sunday, and Nvidia's board has opened an independent investigation, according to Bloomberg. Nvidia said the probe followed reports that Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence effort. The FBI is looking into whether Huang, 63, became the subject of a foreign intelligence operation, according to Reuters.

The New York Times reported that American officials received a briefing saying Alice Huang worked for China's Ministry of State Security, according to CNBC. China has rejected the allegation, calling it a smear. President Donald Trump said on Monday that Jensen Huang told him he "had no idea that she was a spy."

Jensen Huang has been asked to appear at a closed-door session before several House committees meeting jointly, to discuss Nvidia's security protocols. The session is scheduled for Nov. 18, according to Bloomberg.

Sen. Elizabeth Warren asked Huang in June to testify before the Senate Banking Committee about Nvidia's compliance with U.S. export control laws and its business in China.

Running Faster vs Running Longer: Which Should I Focus On First?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Both are key components of a smart training plan—but how you prioritize each one depends on your goal.

Runners have two main paths to choose from when it comes to running goals: run faster or run longer. Both will make you a stronger, fitter athlete—but training for one looks a little different than the other.

Should you focus on running faster to build power and strength, or should you go the distance to test your physical and mental stamina? There’s no right or wrong answer, but understanding the differences between these two approaches can help you make the most of your training.

Like so many questions in running, the answer depends on your current fitness level and your goals. But for most new runners, it’s generally better to start training to run longer.

“I think sometimes runners are quick to jump into trying to run all-out, whatever their goal race is, instead of just working toward being able to feel okay completing the distance first,” says Ruth Atkinson, VDOT-certified McKirdy Trained running coach based in Dallas. That means you probably want to think about the distance you want to cover first, whether you’re vying for your first 5K or have some shorter distances under your belt and are considering a half marathon or marathon.

There’s a saying in running: Start where you are. In other words, don’t put a time goal on a race when you start training. Instead, start training, see how your fitness improves as you get closer to race day, then set a time goal. Some research even indicates that building up your easy mileage will lead to better long-distance run performance compared to focusing on speed workouts: A study published in the Journal of Strength and Conditioning Research in 2021, involving 84 male runners, found a correlation between volume of easy runs—not tempo runs, not interval training—with world-class long-distance running performance scores.

One of the most important benefits of gradually increasing your volume is training your tendons, ligaments, joints, and bones to withstand the repetitive impact of running without overtaxing them, which might lead to overuse injuries. “Starting with distance gives your body a chance to catch up to the muscular capacity you need to develop to stay healthy and move forward,” says New York-based Nike running coach Rebeka Stowe. It’s generally easier and faster for beginners to build aerobic capacity compared to muscle strength, so you have to give your muscles enough time to build that strength.

Once you have a solid aerobic base—the foundational fitness you need to sustain your training—you could shift to more of a speed focus.

Not all running has the same effects on your body. When you’re training for distance, most miles will be at a low-intensity effort. (When it comes to rate of perceived exertion, this would be about a 4 out of 10 effort, or lower, with 10 being all out.) That easy, conversational pace may not feel challenging—although it will help you develop mental toughness by teaching you how to push through fatigue and discomfort as the miles add up—but it’s stimulating important physiological adaptations that will improve your performance at faster paces.

For starters, you’re improving your cardiovascular capabilities. “Your heart is a muscle,” says Stowe. “By running at a slower pace, you’re building that muscle’s capacity to pump blood to the rest of your body so it can handle higher intensity work later.” As your heart’s stroke volume increases with training, it beats less frequently to supply the same amount of blood to the body—meaning it’s becoming more efficient.

Slower runs also train your slow twitch (type I) muscle fibers, which allow you to sustain your pace over longer distances. These muscle fibers have a higher density of mitochondria and greater capillary density, both of which help you maintain your energy level.

In fact, aerobic exercise increases the number of mitochondria in your cells, according to research published in the American Journal of Physiology—Endocrinology and Metabolism, and can increase capillary density by more than 25 percent, an older study in Journal of Atherosclerosis and Thrombosis found. The more mitochondria you have, the more energy you can produce, and the greater your capillary density, the better blood flow to your working muscles. Both of those benefits translate to improved performance at faster speeds.

Speed work, on the other hand, acts almost like strength training, developing your muscles as you work against increased ground reaction forces.

“Challenging your body to run at a faster pace is going to call on more muscle fibers,” says Stowe. In this case, you’re working fast twitch (type II) muscle fibers, which are best suited for short, explosive efforts. These fast-twitch fibers also come into play at the end of a race for your finishing kick.

Picking up the pace also improves your cardiovascular capacity, but in a different way than easy running. Working at a higher intensity increases your VO2 max, “the maximum amount of oxygen that your body can use during exercise,” explains Atkinson. “The more you practice running at this pace, the easier it will be to translate that into holding faster paces for longer periods of time.”

Training for speed will also improve your lactate threshold—the intensity level at which lactate accumulates in the blood faster than you can remove it, leading to fatigue—so you can improve your endurance at faster paces.

To gain these benefits, you can base the pace needed off of your 5K time. If you don’t know your race pace for that distance, you can just go out and run 3.1 miles as hard as you can—it should be an 8 or 9 effort level on a scale of 1 to 10. VO2 max pace is a bit faster than 5K pace, while lactate threshold pace is a bit slower than 5K pace. (Keep in mind speed workouts aren’t always built around just these specific paces. A good training plan will have you holding speeds from mile pace or faster all the way up to marathon pace and slower, depending on your goals.)

Speed training also positively affects the density and structure of your bones, and may potentially prevent age-related bone loss, according to a research review published in 2023 in Sechenov Medical Journal—further bulletproofing your body against overuse injuries.

Another benefit of speed training: It requires a lot less time than building volume, which helps runners who are balancing training with other obligations.

When you train for distance, your workouts will focus on gradually building up the time or mileage you run. Long runs at a conversational pace will be the cornerstone of your plan, along with some moderate-intensity runs and recovery days.

“If you’re running four days a week, you’d have one day of speed and three easy days with higher volume, including a long run,” says Atkinson. Your speed workout would typically include longer intervals at goal pace, adds Stowe. “You should do a good majority of your speed volume at half marathon or marathon pace so that you become more efficient—and more comfortable—holding that pace,” she explains.

The goal, when you’re training to run longer, is to improve your aerobic endurance and teach your body to handle the physical demands of extended activity. As you progress, you’ll increase your weekly mileage to build the stamina you need to go the distance.

Training for speed generally aims to improve your pace over shorter distances. You might run shorter, more intense workouts but with more recovery time between efforts. Let’s say you’re training for a fast 5K race: “You would ease into it by doing 200-meter and 400-meter reps at goal 5K pace, because you’re not going to be able to sustain that pace over longer distances yet,” says Stowe. “As your training continues, you progress to 800s, mile repeats, even 2K repeats—you can work your way up to holding that goal pace in smaller chunks.”

If you’re still running four days a week, your training plan might include three easy runs and a speed workout, or two days with speed and two that are easy, Atkinson says. Your focus will be on maximizing your cardiovascular capacity, running form, and the ability to push your body to sustain those faster efforts. “The quality of your workouts will be a little bit harder, which means the quantity will be a little bit less to keep everything balanced,” she explains.

A good training plan incorporates speed and volume, because your body has different energy systems that respond to different stimuli.

“Think of it like a recipe,” says Stowe. “If you’re supposed to mix salt and sugar into something but you forget to do one, you’re not going to get the desired outcome. The taste is going to be off, right? If you want to optimize your outcome, you have to have speed and volume as part of your training, so you’re developing all of your systems.”

Starting with distance will help you build the foundation you need to develop speed. “If you spend a dedicated amount of time increasing your volume, you’re going to build a pretty robust aerobic system,” says Atkinson. “When you add speed work to that, you’re learning how to turn your legs over faster and become more efficient at faster paces. Then, you have the aerobic system in place to be able to hold the faster paces you can now run.”

For most runners, it’s a cycle. You may spend four to six months training for distance, and then two to four months working on speed; then you layer endurance back in and combine the two.

Both speed and distance training have their own set of benefits, and you can always mix and match, choosing what fits your current goals and lifestyle. Whether you’re training to run faster or longer, each step will bring you closer to becoming the strongest version of yourself.

Adobe unveils new AI tools for Photoshop, Premiere, and more, with MCP support coming in 2027

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Adobe today announced a sweeping set of updates at its annual Adobe MAX conference, focused on giving users more ways to automate complex workflows, edit content, and work across its creative apps. Here are the details.

At the Adobe MAX event today, the company unveiled a series of features, many of them AI-powered or AI-assisted, that will begin rolling out across its suite of creative platforms as early as today.

In Photoshop, the new Relight tool offers a way to non-destructively and dynamically add, reposition, and resize light sources in the composition, while Generative Restylelets users** prompt the platform to generate variations of an image’s style and fine-tune the results using adjustable sliders.

Photoshop is also adding a Quick Compare slider to make it easier to track before-and-after results, and is getting several features in the beta app, including, per Adobe:

Following its acquisition of Topaz Labs, Adobe is also introducing two new AI image enhancement models: Wonder 4, which combines upscaling, denoising, deblurring, and sharpening into a single operation, and Image HDR, which converts standard images into HDR with improved brightness and detail.

Adobe Premiere is also getting generative tools that make it easier to prepare raw files for a project, including Enhance Audio, Separate Crosstalk, and Separate Sound Source. Adobe is also adding a Generative Remove tool to Premiere in beta, “bringing you the power to remove unwanted objects, like boom mics and logos or background distractions, directly on your timeline,” says the company.

Premiere is also getting Color Mode, a new color-grading tool starting today, which “supports everything from quick corrections to more advanced grades while keeping your video as the focus.”

Another interesting tool making its way into Premiere is Generative Media Tool. Here’s Adobe:

The Generative Media Tool brings Generate Video, Generate Sound Effects, Generate Soundscape and Generate Music directly into your timeline in Premiere. You can generate missing b-roll, background music or sound for a scene using context from your edit, then keep refining the result in your timeline. You can also choose from Adobe Firefly and models from partners including Google, Kling, Runway and Luma based on the creative needs of your project.

As for iPhone users who rely on Premiere Mobile, Adobe announced that its newly established partnerships with UMG and WMG will bring hundreds of thousands of licensed music tracks to the app (for non-commercial use) starting today, with users able to explore trending songs, discover featured artists, and create videos using artist-inspired templates.

Premiere mobile is also getting agentic tools soon, including Quick Cuts, which generates a first cut based on the user’s prompt and the materials they add, and an AI Assistant, which helps users find the right editing tools and execute tasks based on natural language instructions.

In Lightroom, the company is adding multiple new tools that make it easier to edit or more quickly fix small imperfections. That includes Glow, which “adds soft, cinematic light to your photos,” Blemish Removal for quick skin fine-tuning, Open Eyes in beta, and an improved and expanded Camera Tethering on Desktop and the iPad, with support for more cameras and advanced shooting controls, plus the ability to connect cameras directly to an iPad for live previews and photo editing.

Adobe is adding new brushes, gradients, patterns, and graphic styles to Illustrator, alongside new Blend controls, which “make it easier to develop and refine designs with a persistent control panel and live preview,” per the company.

As for InDesign, a new Co-editing feature, available in beta, streamlines collaborative work, while the new Flex Layout tool dynamically adjusts content based on the canvas size and artwork. InDesign is also getting Check Document and Rewrite that more quickly “help identify common editorial and accessibility issues while you’re still working.”

While it will not launch until 2027, this feature will enable users to connect Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere, and After Effects to the AI tools and agents they already use.

In practice, this will mean connecting Photoshop to ChatGPT and having them work in tandem to adapt an entire campaign based on new artwork you develop and attach to your prompt.

This integration will also account for other connected tools, so the user can ask ChatGPT to make edits, add new files to a Google Drive folder, update the team on Slack, and so on.

Here’s Adobe on the upcoming MCP integration:

Because it runs locally on your computer, your agent works directly in your native project files, and your layers, vectors, bins and sequences stay editable.

The company notes that MCP integrations will enter private beta in early 2027, and users can sign up here to learn more.

According to Adobe, Firefly is getting even better at helping users organize and edit their raw material for tasks such as turning travel clips into a vlog, creating social media campaigns, or assembling videos from storyboards.

The new Firefly Video lets users organize scenes, assemble clips, add captions, and generate transitions and B-roll, while Firefly Design brings illustration, image editing, animation, and 3D tools into a single canvas.

Adobe is also adding Quick Actions for common editing tasks, Firefly Graph for creating reusable workflows, and Firefly Builder (in private beta) for prototyping websites and apps using natural language.

Firefly’s AI Assistant is also getting new capabilities, including Plan Mode, which lets users review and approve an agent’s proposed actions, and Model Selector, which lets them choose which AI model to use.

Adobe is also previewing Always-on agents, which can handle recurring tasks such as organizing assets, checking quality, and preparing content for different platforms in the background, even when users step away.

Adobe is also expanding its Creative Agent across Creative Cloud, with AI Assistants now available in beta in Illustrator, Premiere, InDesign, After Effects, and Frame.io, as well as the Photoshop beta app.

These assistants allow users to execute complex, multi-step tasks using natural language prompts. In Premiere, for example, users can ask the assistant to apply effects to an entire sequence, track subjects across frames, or adjust color and exposure across multiple clips.

To learn more about these and other features announced at Adobe MAX today, stay tuned to the company’s blog.

I'm Not A "Bowel Normie" & I Still Have Three Healthy Kids

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

My body, once a disintegrating and diseased lump of organs, had made so much life.

I became sick when I was 12. It happened out of nowhere. We were on a family trip to New York City to celebrate New Year’s Eve in Times Square. I stood out on the Statue of Liberty’s crown, sipping the (extravagantly priced) hot chocolate my parents bought me to combat the cold, gazing in awe at the dazzling metropolis, and was suddenly overcome with nausea. That night, I began vomiting, running to the toilet, and developed a high fever. I couldn’t even make it to the window to see the ball drop.

Over the next three years, I spent much of my time in doctor’s offices and bathrooms, with bloating, constipation, diarrhea and bleeding. I was in pain, mostly searing pangs. Who knew when they would hit? I went for X-rays, scopes, breath tests, physical exams; I tried medications, carbon supplements, acupuncture. Steroids worked miraculously at first, then not at all. Experimental drugs went through me completely undigested. I began to evacuate the lining of my own colon; it looked like a thin white snake. My blood count was so low I was repeatedly hospitalized for blood transfusions and iron infusions. It appeared I had very severe ulcerative colitis.

During this time, I was in 7th to 10th grades, the time of first loves, frenemies, tattoos and drivers ed. But as someone with acute bowel disease, my teenage life was increasingly limited. Want a ride home? No thanks. I was worried that whoever was driving would take detours, keeping me away from a toilet. The metro was scary; what if it stalled and I was stuck? I went to school but sat next to the door of the classroom. It was hard for me to go to boy-girl get-togethers: I couldn’t even sit through a movie in someone’s basement, and what if the bathroom was within earshot? The logistics were so difficult, the risk of embarrassment too high. I stopped attending most things.

All teenagers deal with their body changing, with it being out of their control. My body was waging war on me, emitting shameful sounds, smells and sights without my consent. It felt like I, Little Judy, was buried somewhere deep inside this morass of sickly flesh, this thing that all these ancient doctors were probing, trying to decipher. Had I done something to cause this? Was it my fault? I didn’t not-love my body, I hated it.

When my friends were exploring sexuality, I recoiled. For one, it’s hard to think about smooching when you’re doubled down in anguish. Constantly exposed to the medical world, I’d created strong barriers, dissociating from my physical experience.

Not to mention, how could I possibly share what I was going through with anyone? My peers were worried about algebra tests; I was worried about blood tests. My friends gossiped about trying drugs; I was on more drugs than half the school combined! I went to doctors who mentioned the possibility of developing cancer. I felt misunderstood, alone, and so very ugly. I didn’t know how to let anyone get close.

By tenth grade, my life was completely in the toilet, literally. Surgery was offered as an option. Major surgery: they would remove my entire large intestine colon and create a pouch from part of the small intestine that would, hopefully, work as a de facto colon. Doctors kept postponing the procedure, worried about the risks. But I insisted on the operation. I told my parents, if they didn’t take me, I’d take the bus.

So, at 15, I had my entire colon removed. My pouch was amazing; I was not a “bowel normie,” and I couldn’t eat with abandon, but I lived a mostly pain-free life. I remained relatively healthy through my twenties, eventually dating (and making up for lost time!). I got married at age 31 to a man who I was able to open up to about my chronic condition, and who was willing to support my toilet paper habit. But then came the question: was my body robust enough to have a baby? Could I get past all my shame, all my corporeal disappointment, to create a life? And should I be a parent, i.e. would my children end up sick too? I wobbled for two years.

Then, the second I’d decided to maybe even consider trying to get pregnant, my gynecologist enrolled me in a fertility clinic, saying, “People with your surgeries have so much trouble, there’s not a minute to waste.” A few weeks later, I was doing bloodwork at my local clinic so they could send the results to the fertility center. Only, it turned out, as a squealing nurse revealed, I was already pregnant.

The nine months passed seamlessly, bowel-wise, except for some excruciating bouts of gas that had become trapped under my growing belly. My first daughter was born healthy via C-section (recommended, as natural childbirth carried a risk of damaging the pouch). Though I did have a dangerous and frightening ectopic pregnancy a few years later when trying for my second — most likely due to scar tissue from the old surgery — I ultimately ended up having three children which is a miracle, and also, a little insane. My body, once a disintegrating and diseased lump of organs, had made so much life! Never mind years of yoga and pilates, finally, I felt strong. And even, a little bit beautiful.

Judy Batalion is a New York Times bestselling author of award-winning nonfiction and fiction. Her essays have appeared in The New York Times, The Washington Post, and Vogue, among others. Judy has a BA in the History of Science from Harvard, a PhD from the University of London, and has worked as a museum curator and university lecturer. She lives in New York City with her husband and three children.

Not American Or Cheddar: Grab This Cheese For Perfect Breakfast Sandwiches (Thanks, Martha Stewart)

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Practical, budget-friendly, and endlessly versatile, sandwiches are an everyday staple for a reason. Though there's no right or one way to do them, certain combinations have proven to be instant crowd-pleasers. Enter the humble bacon, egg, and cheese, the breakfast of champions and Bobby Flay's favorite late-night snack. It sounds simple enough, but the ingredients you use and how you treat them can be the difference between standard deli fare and Michelin-worthy grub. If you like the sound of the latter option, borrow a tip from the icon herself, Martha Stewart, and use fontina cheese next time you're making one.

Fontina is a must-try Italian cheese. Originating from the Aosta Valley in the Italian Alps, it's a semi-soft cow's milk cheese that's been produced since the 12th century. Its high fat content is responsible for its creamy texture, accompanied by a mild nutty and earthy flavor that intensifies with age. Beyond these already impressive attributes, the thing that makes it so well-suited to a bacon, egg, and cheese is its melting capability. You'll have no trouble finding it in Italian dishes like fonduta alla Valdostana (Italy's answer to fondue) and polenta concia, where it's melted down into a thick, gooey consistency with polenta, milk, and butter. Martha Stewart also pairs fontina with pizza on a pie that features lemon and rosemary (via YouTube).

There are many versions of fontina available, but for the most traditional, pick up Fontina DOP, which is made from raw, whole milk of native Valdostana cattle breeds fed on alpine grass or hay. Because it uses raw milk, it must be aged past the USDA's required 60 days. To put it all together, take a cue from Martha Stewart and use an English muffin as your base. Toast it until golden, then layer on crispy bacon and eggs cooked to your liking. While the eggs are still hot, add a slice or two of fontina and let the residual heat melt the cheese into a creamy, gooey layer.

A bacon, egg, and cheese paired with fontina is already a winning combination, but there are a few ways to make it even better. For quick melting and an extra hit of creamy flavor, place the fontina on your bread and give it a blast under the broiler. You can also pop your bacon under there to get it extra crispy, but hold off on adding the egg until afterward. It's delicate, after all, and can quickly overcook under such intense heat. On that note, keep a close eye on your cheese as it broils — there's a fine line between beautifully melted and golden and burnt beyond saving.

Beyond breakfast sandwiches, you can also use fontina to make another classic: grilled cheese. Given its excellent reputation for meltability, adding fontina to a grilled cheese is practically a no-brainer. You don't have to stop at bread and cheese, either. If you want to put a twist on the classic grilled cheese and tomato soup pairing, tuck in a few slices of tomato and some fresh basil leaves.

In a nod to its Italian heritage, fontina is also a welcome addition to a panini. Your idea of a panini might depend on where you are in the world. In Italy, panino simply means sandwich, and often features a more restrained combination of cured meats, cheese, and vegetables than its U.S. counterpart. So if you want the fontina to shine, take a little inspiration from its homeland. Try layering it with thinly sliced prosciutto, roasted red peppers, and a few leaves of peppery arugula on ciabatta. Add a drizzle of olive oil and a little freshly cracked black pepper and sea salt, then use a panini press until the bread is crisp and the cheese has melted.

A lip balm that ~everyone~ has? “Just money”? And boy, if you get it wrong, they’ll let you know. (It’s in the eyes.)

Liam Coen: "Definitely a curve" to getting Jessie Bates ready this week

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Jaguars made three trades on Monday, but their deal for safety Jessie Bates was the only one that was completed when head coach Liam Coen met with reporters on Monday.

Coen called it a “no-brainer” to make a trade for a player who has shown the “ability to see things before they happen” on the field while also serving as a team captain in the locker room. Bates has been honing those skills for nine seasons, so he’s gained quite a bit of experience but Coen said it will still be “definitely a curve” in terms of getting him acclimated to the Jaguars defense on the fly.

“You’ve seen it done so many ways, right? So many different examples of it being done,” Coen said. “I know he’s pretty intelligent, and he’ll be able to understand a lot of that. It’s more the checks and balances right in between plays, the communication from two by two to three by one, the motions, the checks. Like our verbiage where I would imagine three, cover three, cover four, you know, like the coverages, he’ll understand and know and be able to play those, line up and play. It’s more of the checks and balances and things that go into each play that we’ll have to work through. We’ll take it one day at a time, see what happens.”

Defensive tackles Jordan Davis and Davon Godchaux are the other new members of the Jaguars defense and the team’s staff will be working overtime this week to make sure all of them are up to speed in time to face the Titans on Sunday.

Diana Ross at Home: 10 Photos of the Pop Diva Off-Duty

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

As the breakout star of the Supremes and later a solo sensation, Diana Ross proved herself a force to be reckoned with. The Detroit native’s booming career allowed the singer-slash-actor to fashion her own residences in her own singular style: A statement purple sofa, a shag-lined conversation pit, beaded curtains, and a mirrored piano were among the features that might’ve stood out elsewhere, but all appeared seamlessly at home with The Boss. As she declared in her 1979 anthem of independence: “There’s my chair / I put it there / Everything you see / Is with love and care / It’s my house and I live here.”

Through 12 number-one singles with The Supremes, six number-one hits as a solo artist, a Lifetime Achievement Award from the Recording Academy, and a Presidential Medal of Freedom, Ross apparently managed to maintain a sense of normalcy in her domestic life. “My mom is such a mom,” Ross’s daughter, actor Tracee Ellis Ross, said on The Kelly Clarkson Show. “I had a really regular upbringing.” Read on for a roundup of the icon in her offstage moments.

Ross poses on a floral sofa in her Miami suite in this photo, dated March 17, 1970. It was a big year for the songstress: She performed her final concert as lead singer of The Supremes that January, and her debut solo album was released in June.

The singer unwinds on the sofa during an interview.

When these portraits were snapped, Ross was in the middle of a nine-day run of shows at Miami’s Eden Roc hotel—her official debut solo show after a preview run in Framingham, Massachusetts. “Welcome to the ‘Let’s see if Diana Ross can do it by herself club,’” the vocalist said during the performance, journalist Shirley Eder wrote in a March 20, 1970, issue of The Philadelphia Inquirer. Eder also reported that “Diana takes a long time to unwind after a performance… she screens movies in her suite almost nightly.”

A pregnant Ross leans against her reflective piano.

Ross, eight months pregnant with Tracee in this 1972 photo, leans against the mirrored piano in her Beverly Hills home during an interview about her first film role: Billie Holiday in Lady Sings the Blues, directed by Sidney J. Furie. Ross’s depiction of the jazz singer garnered her a Golden Globe and an Oscar nomination, among other accolades. “She’s the greatest improviser I’ve ever known,” Furie told The Hollywood Reporter in 2021. “Some of the main scenes were improvised…. She was the consummate pro and not a diva at all.”

The musician at home with her daughter Rhonda.

Ross lounges in her Beverly Hills home alongside her eldest child, Rhonda, in 1972. The “Ain’t No Mountain High Enough” singer’s career was launched by Motown founder Berry Gordy, and the two shared a relationship that was romantic as well as professional. Rhonda was born in 1971 as a result of that liaison, but Ross married music executive Robert Ellis Silberstein two months into the pregnancy, and he raised Rhonda as his own. The couple welcomed two more daughters together: Tracee, who was born not long after this photo was taken, and Chudney, who was born in 1975.

Ross at home taping an appearance for an ABC special.

In the mid-1970s, The Wiz star was once again photographed on the purple sofa in her Beverly Hills living area, which was complete with a white-shag-lined conversation pit and a mirror-bedecked fireplace. According to a 1972 New York Times profile of the artist, her 6,833-square-foot home had 30-foot-tall ceilings and an Andy Warhol tapestry of Marilyn Monroe. The grounds also hosted a swimming pool and a tennis court.

Ross with daughters Tracee (left) and Rhonda.

The Ross family’s Beverly Hills pad boasted five bedrooms and nine bathrooms. According to The Los Angeles Times, the property was built in 1964 for art collector Donald Factor. Because its walls maximized space for paintings, it was reportedly known in the neighborhood as “the house with no windows.” The conversation pit abutted a window adorned with a beaded curtain, as shown in the background of this photo of Ross with Tracee and Rhonda. Both daughters followed in their mother’s footsteps as performers: Tracee is an actor (who recently won her first Daytime Emmy for the program Solo Traveling with Tracee Ellis Ross), while Rhonda was nominated for a Daytime Emmy for her role in the soap opera Another World.

Ross, flanked by her daughters, cradles infant Chudney.

A newborn Chudney also made an appearance during the mid-’70s shoot, as seen in this snapshot. Ross and Silberstein divorced in 1977, and the singer went on to marry Norwegian businessman Arne Naess Jr. in 1986. With Naess, she welcomed two sons, Ross and Evan. The “I’m Coming Out” singer and her second husband split in 2000.

In 1978, a newly single Ross welcomed Barbara Walters to her Beverly Hills home for an interview about her career and life as a single mother in front of a window overlooking the backyard pool. “Her success has brought her every material comfort,” Walters narrated in the television special. “Believe it or not, within 24 hours before we came here she completely refurnished the living room.” The television special offered a glimpse of the conversation pit, now done up with dark maroon carpet and a navy sofa with white cushions.

Ross dressed to impress for her birthday party.

The “Upside Down” singer celebrated her 38th birthday with a party at her Beverly Hills residence in March 1982, as seen in this glamorous snapshot. Later that year, she released Silk Electric, her 13th studio album, and was honored with a star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame.

Ross’s daughters were in attendance, as shown in this photo of her bustling birthday party. The 13-time Grammy nominee sold the Beverly Hills property three years later for $1.75 million. These days, the 82-year-old icon lives at Quarry Farm, a property in Greenwich, Connecticut. In 2021, she released her 25th studio album, Thank You.

Dominic Cooper, Stephen Fry, Rupert Everett & Finn Cole Set For True-Story Falklands Dramedy ‘All In The Same Boat’; Playtime & UTA Launch For AFM

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Dominic Cooper (Reacher), Stephen Fry (The Hobbit), Rupert Everett (Shakespeare In Love) and Finn Cole (Peaky Blinders) have been set to star in comedy-drama All In The Same Boat, based on the true story of a British passenger ferry sent to the Falklands War in 1982.

Paris-based Playtime is handling international sales and will co-rep North American sales with UTA Independent Film Group at this week’s American Film Market.

Written and directed by David Stoddart (Lone Star Bull), the film is due to start shooting in summer 2027, the 45th anniversary of the conflict. The synopsis reads: “In 1982, the civilian ferry MV Norland and its crew are requisitioned to carry nearly 1,000 paratroopers to the Falklands War. The forced cohabitation in such close quarters is at first chaotic, especially for Wendy and Frankie, two flamboyant gay stewards who face hostility from the soldiers. But as the ship heads south, Wendy’s piano nights become the heart of the ship and slowly forge an unlikely bond between soldiers and crew.” Related Stories News 'The Perfect Lie' Actor Matthijs Van De Sande Bakhuyzen To Lead AFM-Bound Arctic Thriller 'The Art Of Survival' News Alex Proyas Takes Over Directing Duties On AI-Enabled Historical Drama 'Cortés' Starring Antonio Banderas; Filming Underway -- AFM

Cooper will play the character Frankie. Fry will play Captain Ellerby. Everett will play Commander Esplin-Jones. Finn Cole will be paratrooper Trevor. Watch on Deadline

Additionally, Taz Skylar (One Piece) will play Sergeant Major Mal Simpson, and Scott Ellis Watson (I Swear) will play Joe. The role of Wendy is currently being cast.

The film is co-written with Warren Fitzgerald and Tom Williams, and based on real events and on Fitzgerald’s book All in the Same Boat: The Untold Story of the British Ferry Crew Who Helped Win the Falklands War. It is produced by Christopher Simon of New Sparta Productions, whose credits include Miss You Already and Mafia Mamma and Yorgos Lanthimos’s The Killing of a Sacred Deer.Writer-director David Stoddart said: “I’m thrilled to be working with New Sparta again on All in the Same Boat. What I love about this film is that it’s such an unlikely true story. It’s funny, outrageous at times, but at its heart, it’s incredibly moving. And to have this cast coming together to tell it is so exciting. We’ve got some of the most brilliant British actors playing these wonderfully larger-than-life characters, and I think there’s going to be a lot of fun in bringing them all together on a ship. I want audiences to have an absolute blast watching this film, but also to come away moved by the extraordinary humanity of the people at the centre of it.”Christopher Simon of New Sparta Productions commented: “All in the Same Boat is an absolute blast: a heartfelt true story that will have audiences laughing out loud, reaching for the tissues and dancing in the cinema. It has been the biggest joy to develop it alongside the extraordinary David Stoddart from Warren Fitzgerald’s amazing book and a cast bursting with international treasures.”Sébastien Beffa, Managing Director, Playtime added: “The whole Playtime team is thrilled. It’s a story firmly anchored in its period, yet strikingly timely in the themes it explores and in its characters, who deeply moved us when we first read the script. This is exactly the kind of project with real crossover potential. Christopher and Sophie make for a terrific producing team, and we are big believers in David as a director.”Cooper is represented by Markham, Froggatt & Irwin and WME. Fry is repped by Hamilton Hodell and CAA. Everett is represented by The Artists Partnership. Cole is represented by UTA, Independent Talent Group, and Sloane, Offer. Skylar is with Independent Talent Group, Common Ground Entertainment and Verve. Watson is represented by Hunwick Associates and The Artists Partnership.

Taz Skylar as Sergeant Major Mal Simpson (One Piece, Boiling Point)Skylar is represented by Independent Talent Group, Common Ground Entertainment and VerveScott Ellis Watson as Joe (I Swear, Ghosts: The Possession of Button House)Watson is represented by Maryam Hunwick at Hunwick Associates and Taylor Jefferson at The Artists PartnershipTBC for the role WendyCrew:Writer / Director: David StoddartStoddart is represented by Independent Talent and UTAWriters: Warren FitzGerald & Tom WilliamsProducer: Christopher SimonCo-Producer: Sophie LipseyExecutive Producers: Orlando Figg, Jerome Booth, Maggie MonteithCinematographer: John LeeProduction Designer: Amanda McArthurCasting Director: Nancy BishopABOUT THE PRODUCTIONALL IN THE SAME BOAT is written and directed by David Stoddart (Lone Star Bull), co-written with Warren Fitzgerald and Tom Williams, and based on real events and on Fitzgerald’s book All in the Same Boat: The Untold Story of the British Ferry Crew Who Helped Win the Falklands War. It is produced by Christopher Simon of New Sparta Productions (UK), the company behind Miss You Already and Mafia Mamma and co-financier of Yorgos Lanthimos’s The Killing of a Sacred Deer.
