GTA 6 PS5 Pro Features Revealed, 30fps Target on Both Consoles

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Improved clarity and ray traced shadows on PS5 Pro

Rockstar Games has today revealed what sort of benefits playing GTA 6 on a PS5 Pro will provide.

No matter whether you play on a base PS5 or a PS5 Pro, though, the game will target 30 frames-per-second, a new post on the developer's website confirms:

Grand Theft Auto VI has been fully developed from the ground up to provide the best possible experience for the current generation of consoles, targeting 30 frames per second while pushing both platforms to their absolute limits to bring the vast and rich world of Leonida to life — while maintaining the density and visual fidelity of all its environments and inhabitants.

The improvements are slim on PS5 Pro; the article mentions "improved clarity" thanks to PSSR, and there'll be ray traced shadows that are only available on the upgraded console.

There is no 60fps mode, or even a 40fps mode for GTA 6. This also applies to the Xbox Series X|S versions.

Guide GTA 6 Release Times and Preload: When Can You Start Playing?

The blog post then talks up the game's use of the PS5 DualSense controller, making use of haptic feedback, far better audio, and improved load times. It concludes by calling PS5 the best place to play GTA 6.

As of today, the game is available to preload worldwide on PS5 ahead of the full 19th November launch. Please refer to our GTA 6 Release Times and Preload guide for more information. How do you feel about there being no 40fps or 60fps performance modes in GTA 6? Let us know in the comments below.

Cassano: ‘Allegri will end up at Fiorentina with Paratici, those two always land on their feet’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Antonio Cassano has predicted that Massimiliano Allegri will join Fiorentina after his time at Napoli, reuniting with former Juventus director Fabio Paratici.

According to Calciomercato.com, the former Italy forward launched another attack on the Napoli coach on Viva El Futbol, following Sunday’s 1-1 draw with Lazio.

“He keeps making a mess of things, but nobody says anything and nobody sacks him,” Cassano said. “He says things that make no sense and doesn’t manage his squad. It’s a scandal to see such a big points gap between Lazio and Napoli when you think about the two wage bills.”

The draw left Napoli 14 points behind leaders Lazio. Allegri has been under heavy pressure after defeats to Juventus and Porto, and Aurelio De Laurentiis visited the training ground last week.

Cassano believes Allegri’s best days are behind him, and he expects Paratici, now Fiorentina’s sporting director, to come to his rescue.

“Allegri’s time has passed, and it has been for at least seven or eight years. He doesn’t win any more. But don’t worry, after the flop at Napoli someone else will give him another chance, and I bet it’ll be Fabio Paratici. Trust me, it’ll definitely happen. They didn’t part on the best of terms, but he’ll take him to Fiorentina. Those two always land on their feet.”

Allegri and Paratici worked together at Juventus during the coach’s first spell in Turin, winning five consecutive Scudetti. Paratici left for Tottenham in 2021, around the time Allegri returned to the Bianconeri.

Fiorentina are currently coached by Paolo Vanoli and are fighting to stay out of the relegation zone.

‘Nobody Wants This’ Star Timothy Simons Reveals Biggest Season 3 Concern and Looks to Season 4: “They Burned One Another — So They’re Even”

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The 'Veep' actor also opens up about his co-stars and creator weighing in on Adam Brody's Gaza comments, and how being on the Jewish show has shaped his own views on religion.

[This story contains spoilers from Nobody Wants This season two.]

When Timothy Simons and Justine Lupe found out that this would be such a big season for their Nobody Wants This duo Sasha and Morgan, respectively, Simons said he and his co-star had some key concerns.

“We are very limb-forward people. We have a lot of arms,” he tells The Hollywood Reporter with a laugh. “Even weeks before we left [to film the] New York [episode], I said to our director, ‘Hey, can we talk about the blocking for this? Because we just don’t want to look like muppets.'” Related Stories Movies 'Steps' Review: A Stellar Voice Cast Including Amanda Seyfried, Ali Wong and Bette Midler Gets Stuck in a Tortured Revisionist Fairy Tale TV 'Nobody Wants This' Stars Hint at Season 4 and Declare Timothy Simons "the Lead of the Show Now"

When it came time to film the seventh episode of season two, “Watch What Happens,” Simons said he and Lupe took the trust and responsibility of their story getting such a spotlight seriously. The result was a bottle episode that is getting rave reviews for taking Sasha and Morgan out of Los Angeles and landing them in a bed together, only to blow up their romance shortly after when Esther (Jackie Tohn), Sasha’s estranged wife, reveals she and Sasha also slept together. The finale leaves the trio, once again, in a love triangle. But it’s slightly different this time around.

“It’s a pretty mature choice for Sasha to be like, ‘This might be the right thing, but it’s the wrong time.’ I also think there was a fair amount of maturity in what Morgan did after years and years of not trying and running from things. So I don’t know which way it’s going to go,” he says of how season three ends, now that the hit Netflix series has been officially renewed for season four.

Below, the Veep actor shares his hopes for next season (the writers room has been up and running), and weighs in on co-star Adam Brody’s Gaza comments, how the show has shaped his views on religion and why Nobody Wants This is not likely to bring such conversations to the Roklov dinner table. “Adam and Erin are both immensely passionate about the views they’ve spoken to. I know they have spoken personally about it before. It’s always respectful,” he says.

So whose idea was it to dye your hair blonde for the season?

I had done it for fun for the Emmys a couple of years ago, when we were there with the show after the first season. All the writers saw it sort of separately and each individually had the idea to write that in. Then they all got together and realized they all had that same thought, so they wrote it in and I had to keep it.

Were you over being a blonde by the end of filming?

I actually loved it and thought it fit the vibe, because Sasha is going through something. Although I wasn’t going through something, I could definitely see how it would fit someone like Sasha going through something. And I will say, if you have hair as short and dark as mine, it requires more upkeep than you think and is a bit of a continuity nightmare. I didn’t get sick of it through the entire season; I really did like it. But after, I did get sick of the upkeep so I’m back to brunette. I’m rocking my real self now and it feels good.

When did you find out this was going to be such a big season for Sasha and Morgan (Justine Lupe)? Can you take me inside that conversation with your creator/showrunners when they first laid it out for you?

Justine and I went into the writers room. They had outlined the entire season and pitched it out to Netflix, and Netflix OK’d it. We do that every year — we sit down and they bring us through the broad strokes. We talk about it a lot and throw out ideas that they can either take or leave. So it was in the writers room early on when we found out.

We’re a 10-episode show and they told us that episode seven was going to be dedicated to just the two of us. As a performer, that feels incredible that they have that amount of trust in Justine and I to hand over responsibility of a full episode just to us. That is immensely flattering. I think we both had the same reaction. We took that seriously. We took that trust and responsibility seriously.

That episode is getting raves for being like a mini rom-com bottle episode. You and Justine are close friends. What was it like to play out Sasha and Morgan’s sexual tension, and eventually sleep together?

We’ve always had similar styles, or at least similar thought processes on performance, which I think is why we get along so well and have chemistry. We have a lot of mutual friends. There was a lot of trust in Justine, at least on my side, from very early on.

We are very limb-forward people. We’re very lanky. We have a lot of arms. (Laughs.) And we just didn’t want to look like muppets when we ended up kissing. So we talked a lot to our director, Richard Shepard, who is incredible. Even weeks before we left for New York I was like, “Hey, can we talk about the blocking for this? Because we just don’t want to look like muppets.”

Because the moment they kiss, if that moment doesn’t work, then none of it works. You built it all up to that. And if we look like muppets, it would undercut the whole thing. But it’s our jobs and we’re close, and we joked around about it a lot. But both of us are professionals about it.

Did you see the twist coming of Sasha and Esther (Jackie Tohn) reuniting?

I didn’t. I feel like hooking up with your ex is a thing that has happened to a lot of people. You can’t just all of a sudden shake off a 20-year relationship and not have that come up again. I didn’t necessarily see it coming, but that felt like something that does naturally happen sometimes. So it did take me by surprise a little bit. But in a good way. Conflict and confrontations are good for drama!

The finale ends unresolved about who he is going to pick, if anyone. Where would you say his heart lies by the end of the season?

I’ve mentioned this to Justine before. Justine is such an incredible performer and can access such an immense vulnerability — especially in that scene in the parking garage. There were times as an actor that I was like, “Can we just fix this for her so she doesn’t have to feel like this?!” But there was a fair amount of maturity that I didn’t even realize until I watched the season. It’s a pretty mature choice for him to be like, “This might be the right thing, but it’s the wrong time.” I don’t think he’s ever said that before. He very much went along with whatever anyone else wanted for him, and so I do think it was a mature choice for him to make to be like, “It seems like not the right thing in either direction.”

So I don’t know which way it’s going to go. But it does seem like he is a little bit like, “I have to back off of both of these things for a little bit.”

Do you think Morgan could be mature enough to get over being burned by Sasha, if he does want to pursue a relationship with her?

I also think there was a fair amount of maturity in what Morgan did after years and years of not trying and running from things. And being willing to take a risk that she’s never taken before and not half-assing anything. I feel like if she is taking steps in that direction, and there could be a world. Also, I would throw in that they kind of burned one another. She kind of burned him when she ran away and he kind of burned her when she said no. So they are still at an even point.

We heard the writers room has been up. Erin Foster was recently said on Dax Shepard’s podcast that they were writing a role for him. Have any ideas been shared with you yet? [Note: This interview occurred before the official season four renewal.]

I would absolutely love to do more of it. I’ve enjoyed the cast and crew and writers and directors of this show immensely. So doing more of it? I’m 100 percent in favor for. But I’m not being cagey when I tell you that I don’t have any idea what they’re talking about, because I haven’t gone in to do the sit down yet.

What decisions would you like to see Sasha making in season four?

The writers are always coming up with better things than me. I have my personal feelings, but they will always come up with better ideas. Either way, he and Esther reconciling… clearly, they do love one another. They built a life together, so you’d like to see them work through that. Maybe they slip back into old habits and all that work he did to put himself together a little bit more goes by the wayside.

And I love the idea of two weirdos like Sasha and Morgan finding each other and that working out, but maybe that’s too messy with the family. There are pros and cons on either side, so I’m interested to see what the writers come up with.

This season got a lot of attention for two things. First, because of the big endings for both Noah (Adam Brody) and Joanne (Kristen Bell), and you and Morgan. But also because of your creator Erin Foster and co-stars responding to Brody’s Gaza comments. Do you feel like there is a responsibility for your writers and cast to speak out because the show is a Jewish show? How do you feel about it, and how do you feel about being asked about it?

Adam and Erin are both immensely passionate about the views they’ve spoken to. And I do think the way Erin said it… they work together really well. I know they have spoken personally about it before. It’s always respectful, and that’s one of the great things about Adam is that he is kind and thoughtful and empathetic and will talk to anybody, even if their views don’t align with his, to try to fight for a safer world for everybody. That’s what’s underneath all of it.

Erin said the show is a love letter to Judaism. After playing a Jewish character on this show, how has that shaped or informed your views on Judaism?

I’ve never been a religious person. I didn’t grow up religious. I had an Irish-Catholic grandmother, but religion was not a huge part of my family or my life coming into the show. But I will say, my dad passed away recently…

Thank you. There was a moment when I got back to Los Angeles after the [funeral] service; we have neighbors who we’ve been good friends with since our kids were in kindergarten together, and they invited me to go to Shabbat at the temple they go to in our neighborhood. There’s a mourner’s circle [the Kaddish] and it was incredible to go to a temple and be welcomed in and be able to say my dad’s name out loud and a rabbi told me, “May his memory be a blessing.”

Even as a non-religious person — I’m certainly not a scholar — there are a lot of rituals surrounding death and mourning and grief that I have learned through this experience of learning more about Judaism that even if I am not religious, have given me a lot of comfort.

Shortly after my dad died, my college roommate died of brain cancer, so I’m in a mourner’s circle saying two names. You realize why religion has been around so long as a guide through moments of grief and to try to find some sense of comfort. That is something that, albeit, I did now know coming in[to this show]. There has been a lot of stuff I learned that has helped me in my day-to-day life because of this experience.

I’m really sorry about your friend and about your dad.

Thank you, I appreciate it. It’s been a little bit of a rough summer, but we’re working through it.

Nobody Wants This isn’t a political show. It’s a feel-good show. Have you had conversations with the writers about if the show would get more current as it goes, or is that not the ethos of the show? Will the extended Roklov family ever be arguing over the war at the dinner table?

Yeah, that has not been the language of this show so far, and I would also throw back to you that the writers always know better than me. If they want to find a way into that, [they will]. Just like I said before about a love triangle, they are going to know how to best attack it.

Your kids [14-year-old twins] love Nobody Wants This. When will you show them Veep?

I actually, for a very long time, had not appeared in a single project that they could have watched. (Laughs.) Now we are at a point where with some outliers, we can pretty much show them everything. We have a lot of movie nights as a family where we’ll show them Rear Window and Mad Max: Fury Road and John Wick. So I do think they are ready to watch Veep. However, there has to be a conversation about, “This is not language that you use in school or with teachers, or in any sort of important situation.”

Would you want to do a Veep revival, or is the real world still getting in the way of that?

Would I love to be in the room with all of those people again? The answer is 100 percent, yes. But we also live in a world that is un-satirizable. The show would be completely different and honestly, what are we going to do that’s dumber than what’s happening? We live in a world that’s beyond parody, so how could we possibly parody it? Would I love to work with those people again? That would be amazing. But I don’t know how it would fit.

I’m glad the show has stayed relevant and people still enjoy it and still see a reflection of the world now, even though we made it so long ago. It’s been six or seven years since it went off the air. That’s amazing. I would sacrifice a little bit of the longtail of the show if the world were a little bit less chaotic. I would really like for fewer people to tell me Veep was still relevant if the world was a little better.

Nobody Wants This season two is now streaming all episodes on Netflix.

The HDMI port settings you should change for the best picture quality

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Don't just adjust the settings on your TV. Your HDMI port settings should also be tweaked.

Most smart TVs aren't optimized for home viewing out of the box. That might sound strange, given that home audiences are usually the main target. Often, though, TVs are given overly conservative default settings, or else they're set to modes that might catch your eye in the store but actively sabotage image quality. There's a reason why Filmmaker Mode had to be invented.

This extends right down to HDMI ports. You'd think ports wouldn't need settings at all, what with the notion of plug-and-play. But they do exist, and the truth is that if you've never changed them, your TV and its connected devices probably haven't been operating at their full potential.

Typically, you only need to turn on a setting called Enhanced for each of your inputs. TV makers do put their own spin on matters, though, so keep your eyes open. In fact, it might be worth setting aside 15 minutes just to check out all the menu options to improve your 4k TV's picture quality.

HDMI inputs frequently default to a "standard" mode intended to maximize compatibility with anything you might plug in. In theory, this sounds like a great idea. However, I've never once encountered a compatibility problem, and the tradeoff is a bottleneck on your most advanced hardware, such as game consoles and Blu-ray players. It's probably high time TV makers take the opposite approach.

In the interim, you can fix this on many TVs (such as Google-based Sony and Hisense models) by locating HDMI input options and flipping each input to Enhanced, or some variation of that label. Effectively what you're doing is unlocking the full bandwidth of each port, which is likely necessary to run features like Dolby Vision or refresh rates over 60Hz. You'd think those would be a given in 2026, as some TVs now support Dolby Vision 2, HDR10+ Advanced or refresh rates up to 240Hz (like Samsung's Frame Pro and OLED TVs).

It gets even more confusing. Some TVs may offer multiple Enhanced tiers, such as one for HDR and another for those high refresh rates. If that's the case, you'll have to prioritize based on what you have connected. PCs and consoles should automatically be assigned the best refresh rates (including Variable Refresh Rate sync), but media streamers and Blu-ray players simply need HDR, since they're unlikely to push past 60Hz anyway.

TVs from LG, Sony, Samsung and others may surface a feature known as HDMI Deep Color. Introduced alongside HDMI 1.3, this simply extends color depth to the levels needed for HDR. Realistically, no consumer TV can take advantage of anything more than 10-bit color. That's fine, though, since it provides around 1.07 billion shades — way more than the human eye can consciously distinguish, allowing for extremely fine gradations.

Deep Color should, ideally, flip on automatically whenever you plug in a compatible device. If you suspect something is wrong, you should typically be able to force it on. You can do this on an LG TV by going to Settings > General > External Devices > HDMI settings > HDMI Deep Color. If you're offered a choice of bits, feel free to choose anything over 8-bit, which is just SDR (Standard Dynamic Range).

If you're connecting a PC or console, keep an eye out for chroma subsampling options. This technology compresses color data for the sake of efficiency and usually isn't a big deal if you're just watching a movie or TV show. With PCs and consoles, however, switching to uncompressed 4:4:4 can go a long way towards making text more legible.

An ode to Jens Lehmann: The maddest of the maverick keepers

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

While it’s a cliche to suggest that all goalkeepers are inherent mentalists, former Arsenal stopper Jens Lehmann certainly lived up to the stereotype. In fact, he took the legend of the maverick goalkeeper to a place where none have dared to venture since.

In a time before the technical skills of Manuel Neuer brought the sweeper-keeper role into fashion, it was a commonly held opinion that the best goalies were usually the biggest mentalists.

Before precision with the ball at one’s feet and chance creation were the remit of a goalkeeper, it was the man who could put his head in front of a flying knee, his face in the way of a stinging shot or his outstretched arms into a crunching challenge who won the most hearts in the stands.

The legend of the maverick goalkeeper had its roots in the story of Bert Trautmann breaking his neck in the 1956 FA Cup final but really took off in their eighties with the arrival of Rene Higuita and Jose Luis Chilavert on the scene.

But if they made eccentric goalkeeping the preserve of South American football, they were rivalled by a generation of rebellious and bullish stoppers in Europe which included Oliver Kahn, Fabien Barthez and the inimitable Jens Lehmann.

While it would be a criminal underestimation of their talents to suggest these keepers were known only for their madcap antics, they were certainly free spirits and staunch nonconformists who had reputations for being unpredictable for a reason.

Kahn was once sent off while playing for Bayern Munich against Hansa Rostock after going up for a corner and punching the ball into the opposition net with both hands.

Barthez famously allowed Paulo Di Canio to score while recreating one of the signature dance moves from Saturday Night Fever, while Lehmann once saw red while at Borussia Dortmund for shoving team-mate Marcio Amoroso, an error of judgement which made him the first Bundesliga goalkeeper to be sent off four times in his career.

If Barthez was known more for his pre-match rituals and unnecessarily risky dribbling, Kahn and Lehmann were men who seemed driven by a deep reserve of internal anger. Rivals for the No.1 jersey at international level with Germany, they also seemed to compete over who could be most full of rage on the pitch.

Kahn was famous for squaring up to strikers, grabbing adversaries by the neck and shoving people indiscriminately – team-mates and opponents alike – while Lehmann was more likely to seethe under the surface before exploding into a screaming fit or, where he deemed it appropriate, some sly act of retaliation.

That was how he came to earn the nickname ‘Mad Jens’ at Arsenal, an epithet he would live up to with the ultimate moment of madness at the Stade de France in May 2006.

There were warning signs that Lehmann’s simmering rage could be self-destructive well before that fateful night in Paris. The most famous was towards the end of Arsenal’s ‘Invincibles’ season of 2003-04, in the game which would see Arsene Wenger’s team crowned Premier League champions at White Hart Lane.

Though the Gunners managed to get the draw they needed to win the league that day, they would have had all three points were it not for a baffling miscalculation from the man between the sticks.

Having come under pressure from Robbie Keane at a corner, Lehmann grabbed his opponent by the head to concede a needless penalty which Keane duly scored.

In truth, Lehmann always seemed more committed to his maverick status than any other goalkeeper.

Speaking to the official Bundesliga YouTube channel in 2015, former Dortmund manager Matthias Sammer said of Lehmann: “He wasn’t the easiest to work with. He was a real individual.”

There was always an air of unorthodoxy around the Essen-born stopper, while he was often characterised as uncompromising, argumentative and even supercilious behind closed doors.

While the rage of his rival Kahn manifested itself as a form of hyper-intense group leadership, Lehmann was more of a renegade with an incredible eye for the direction of the game.

At its best, his individualism was expressed in the form of an authoritative, incontestable command of his area, or even through a goalscoring cameo at a vital time in one particular Ruhr derby.

At its worst, it was expressed through his shaky disciplinary record; in Lehmann’s last season at Dortmund he received four yellow and two red cards, this only a year or so after receiving a four-match ban for booting SC Freiburg midfielder Soumaila Coulibaly off the ball.

When Lehmann was in his haughty pomp, he was a goalkeeper fit to marshal the Invincibles, even if that very same season he was charged with improper conduct for his confrontational behaviour during the ‘Battle of Old Trafford’.

He was a keeper whose incredible reflex saves and in-game intelligence saw Arsenal ship a mere 26 league goals that term, just as he would later go 853 minutes without conceding – chalking up 10 clean sheets – en route to the Champions League final in 2006.

Unfortunately, that final was defined by the self-destructive side of Lehmann’s personality rather than his masterful skill.

Watching it back now, his sending off after 18 minutes makes for truly fascinating viewing.

Lehmann: "As the referee showed me the red card, I felt like the loneliest person on the planet; I could not even muster the strength to complain. This could not be true, I thought, burying my face in my hands." pic.twitter.com/KKy6U2ByQb

In hindsight, so early on in the game, the rational approach would have been to delay Samuel Eto’o as long as possible and pray for the covering run of Kolo Toure to come quick enough, but in the split second after Ronaldinho’s threaded pass, Lehmann’s individual instincts kick in.

He comes screaming out of his box with his eyes on the ball, neither narrowing the angles nor complicating the finish. Instead he is caught in a losing foot race with Eto’o, and soon finds himself trudging miserably off the pitch.

There are some who might argue that Lehmann had only a moment to react to the situation and simply made the wrong decision, with little more to read into it.

Nonetheless, there is something striking about the sight of the supreme individualist stranded and alone in a contest he cannot win.

Whether or not it was Lehmann’s rebellious streak which made him reach out and topple Eto’o as opposed to letting the Cameroonian get the better of him, the German’s reputation proceeded him as he received the first-ever red card in a Champions League final.

He had taken the legend of the maverick goalkeeper to its logical conclusion and – while there have been many lesser goalies who have tried to pass themselves off as mavericks subsequently – it’s hard not to feel that Lehmann’s iconic sending-off is the reason why eccentric stoppers haven’t been in high demand since.

New Long-Haul Era: Lufthansa Receives 1st A350-1000 & 700th Airbus Jet Overall

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Lufthansa has officially taken delivery of its first example of the Airbus A350-1000. The Cologne-based German flag carrier and Star Alliance founding member already flies the standard Airbus A350-900 model, and it is now starting a new era of long-haul flying as the stretched A350-1000 enters the fray. This delivery had extra significance as it also represented the airline's 700th Airbus jet overall.

The momentous occasion was marked, as such milestones often are, with a ceremony at Airbus's facilities at Toulouse Blagnac Airport (TLS) in the southwest of France. Wednesday will subsequently see the jet, which has been painted in Lufthansa's special centenary livery, undertake a ferry flight from Toulouse to Lufthansa's hub at Munich Airport (MUC) ahead of its entry into service in December.

Lufthansa confirmed in a statement that it released earlier today that its first Airbus A350-1000 bears the registration D-AIFA and the name 'Deutschland' (German for 'Germany'). As seen above, data from Flightradar24 shows that the jet concluded its pre-delivery flight test program last week, operating a 64-minute flight out of Toulouse on November 6. It did so under the French registration F-WZNY.

By the time it enters service, D-AIFA will have been fitted with Starlink WiFi, making it the first jet in the long-haul Lufthansa Group fleet to feature the new high-speed inflight connectivity solution. Carsten Spohr, the Chief Executive Officer of the Lufthansa Group, welcomed the fact that the A350 "uses up to 30% less fuel per passenger, while at the same time offering significantly lower unit costs." He added that:

"With our first A350-1000, Lufthansa is taking delivery of the world’s most modern long-haul aircraft. Our guests can look forward to the new Allegris premium cabin in all travel classes."

The delivery of the first stretched Airbus A350 to Lufthansa is the latest milestone in a long and healthy working relationship between the German flag carrier and the transnational European planemaker that dates back half a decade. On this note, Spohr said that "as the world’s largest Airbus customer, we are taking delivery of our 700th Airbus [jet] exactly 50 years after the first Airbus delivery to Lufthansa."

Today, Airbus planes form the backbone of both the narrowbody and widebody fleets at Lufthansa, which continues to fly jets from the A320, A320neo, A330, A340, A350, and even A380 families. Historically, the Lufthansa Group notes that it "has operated aircraft from every Airbus aircraft family and has served as a launch customer on numerous occasions." The A350-1000 marks the latest chapter.

Lufthansa Group Chief Executive Officer Carsten Spohr was joined at the handover ceremony by Lars Wagner, the CEO of Commercial Aircraft at Airbus, with the presence of these high-ranking executives underlining the importance of this maiden delivery. The next step will be to ferry D-AIFA from Toulouse to Munich, with this set to take place on November 11. Shortly thereafter, the aircraft will enter service.

Lufthansa's first A350-1000 has arrived with a special 100th anniversary livery, but how much paint does this livery use?

As it stands, this entry into service is set to come in the form of a long-haul rotation from Munich to Tokyo Haneda Airport (HND) in Japan. This has been penciled in for Thursday, December 3, 2026. Further Asian destinations are set to be added to Lufthansa's growing A350-1000 network in the form of Singapore Changi Airport (SIN) on December 12 and Shanghai Pudong International (PVG) on December 18.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

The Lufthansa Group has placed a grand total of 15 firm orders to date, of which the first ten will be used to serve Lufthansa's long-haul ops out of its Munich Airport hub. The remaining 14 examples of the type are set to be delivered by 2030, with Lufthansa saying that "the entry into service of the new A350-1000 is part of the largest fleet modernization program in the history of the Lufthansa Group."

GTA 6 Is Now The Most Pre-Ordered Game Ever

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

It was always safe to assume that GTA 6 would sell millions of copies before release day thanks to excited fans of the series placing pre-orders. Now we have data not only confirming exactly that, but also that GTA 6 has become the most pre-ordered game ever with nine days still to go.

That's according to the latest data shared by Sensor Tower. According to the data analysis site, GTA 6 has been pre-ordered 9.69 million times so far across PS5 and Xbox Series X|S. That will net Rockstar and Take-Two Interactive almost $1 billion in revenue before anyone has even played the game, and means Cyberpunk 2077's long-standing record has been broken.

One game occupying all three medal positions simultaneously.

Cyberpunk set the record for most pre-orders ever almost exactly six years ago when eight million of you committed to the game before it had been released. A lot of you were left disappointed due to the fact that Cyberpunk then went on to have one of the most infamously disastrous launches in gaming history. It has redeemed itself multiple times over since then, and the odds of GTA 6 falling foul of the same fate are basically zero.

According to the latest data shared by Sensor Tower, GTA 6 has been pre-ordered 1.98 million times in the last week alone, clearly due to the hype for the game really starting to ramp up with less than two weeks to go until launch. That almost certainly means GTA 6 will rack up another two million pre-orders between now and release day, amassing a record that may never be broken.

Sensor Tower's data also reveals how many of you have pre-ordered on PS5 vs. Xbox Series X|S. The split has been a hotly debated topic, with stats claiming PS5 pre-orders were outselling their Xbox equivalent at a rate of eight-to-one. Turns out things aren't that bad for Xbox, although the split is far from even. 75% of GTA 6 pre-orders have been placed on PS5, so PlayStation has three times as many people prepping for what will likely be the biggest launch of all time compared to its Xbox rival.

You might be wondering who on Earth still doesn't know about GTA 6 by this point, at least when it comes to people who might want to buy it ready for release day. You'd be surprised. While news about it has dominated the video game space for pretty much all of 2026, and it has broken containment to a degree, there are still people out there who will be playing on November 19 who either don't know GTA 6 is coming, or for whom it isn't front of mind, so they haven't pre-ordered it yet. This last big marketing push, which includes an entire range of merch at the high-end brand Selfridges, is for them and will ensure GTA 6 continues to sell millions of copies before it has even been released.

Tom Henderson thinks it'll be "easy' for Grand Theft Auto 6 to generate $1 billion in an hour from pre-orders alone.

Hyperlite Mountain Gear Unbound Ultralight Backpack Review

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The updated Hyperlite Mountain Gear Unbound is one of the best lightweight backpacks on the market for any adventure. The new Dyneema fabric is extremely durable, and the large side pockets are some of the most functional we’ve ever used.

Large side pockets. These pockets are big enough to fit large bottles and other items within reach, while holding them securely enough for scrambling

Not for everyone. The Unbound misses some features that some hikers require, such as load lifters and a hydration port.

Big improvements to an already great ultralight backpack. New fabric and a better frame elevate the Unbound by boosting durability and reducing weight. Courtesy of the excellent pockets, it might be HMG’s best backpack.

Years ago, I reviewed what was then a brand new addition to the Hyperlite Mountain Gear (HMG) lineup of multi-day backpacks. It was called the Unbound, and although I initially had my doubts, it eventually became my all-time favorite backpack. Although it appeared similar to the brand’s core three packs (the Southwest, Windrider, and Junction), a few small tweaks significantly improved its utility.

Now, Hyperlite has overhauled the Unbound with a new Dyneema fabric and some other design improvements. Changes aren’t always upgrades, of course. Three years after falling in love with the original Unbound, would the 2026 version — with its fancy new fabric, burlier frame, and lower weight — become a new favorite? Let’s see how it all shakes out.

New Dyneema fabric puts durability where it matters, including the bottom of the Unbound.

MSRP: $420 Weight: 29 ounces (size M) Available sizes: XS, S, M, L, T (torso sizes: 15”-21+”) Volume: 55 liters internal, 9 liters external (40L version available) Max. recommended load: 40 pounds Pockets: 1 main compartment, 1+ front mesh, 2 hip-belt pockets, 2 side pockets, bottom mesh Frame: Two contoured aluminum stays Material: Dyneema Woven Composite 3.9 Country of origin: Mexico

The Unbound is designed for thru-hikers and backpackers who want to move efficiently. While HMG’s other backpacks have remained popular on long trails for over a decade and are worth a look, the Unbound refines the long-distance focus. Subtle yet substantial tweaks to HMG’s winning formula unlock significant advantages for those who spend most of the day on their feet, moving forward.

More versatile pockets are the most obvious difference, but flexible compression and the removable hip belt are other improvements. Ultimately, the Unbound aligns with HMG’s philosophy centering on gear that is lightweight, simple, and durable enough to withstand multiple long thru-hikes.

Read Next – How to Choose a Backpack for Thru-Hiking

The Unbound was with me for a lot of butt-scooting over granite boulders. Photo cred: aeigenbrot

I would love to be wrapping up a SOBO thru-hike right now, carrying the new Unbound, ripe with a sweaty funk and ringed with salt. But alas, I am at home. Still, I managed to put my backpack through its paces, carrying more weight than a typical ultralight-inclined thru-hiker, and bashing it against a plentitude of rough boulders.

On a peak-bagging trip to the Wind River Range, I loaded it above the maximum recommended 40-pound limit and scrambled across miles of talus and steep boulderfields. It also carried all of my stuff during random Colorado overnighters and several SAR missions. Heavy loads, awkward loads, almost empty, on trail, scrambling, rain, snow, sunshine. It may not have been a long thru-hike, but it’s been a good summer, and the Unbound was with me all the way.

Big side pockets and customizable side compression are two of the Unbound’s distinguishing features. Photo cred: aeigenbrot

Dyneema construction: HMG’s new Dyneema Woven Composite (DWC) is a more durable, yet lighter, reimagining of their classic Dyneema Composite Hybrid (DCH). Hyperlite packs were already on the brawnier side of ultralight, and this new fabric takes it a step further. It’s also waterproof when new and won’t absorb moisture, so your stuff is well protected from the elements. However, it’s probably wise to use a pack liner as extra insurance.

Two-stay frame: Two removable aluminum stays sit vertically on the back panel. These are contoured and help maintain a slight air gap between my mid-back and backpack. Removing them saves roughly 2 ounces. The thin foam sewn into the back panel also adds some rigidity and comfort.

Large side pockets: These two pockets are larger than average and easily accommodate two Smartwater bottles each with room to spare. Heck, two classic Nalgenes will fit. A cinch cord helps keep items secure.

Dual-access mesh pocket: The standard large mesh pocket gets a slight tweak with an additional access point a few inches above the bottom. While still secure, this makes it easier to find small items that may have sunk to the bottom.

Removable hip belt: The Unbound’s hip belt is completely removable yet securely held in place behind the lumbar pad via hook-and-loop. You can shave 6 ounces by leaving it at home, or replace a damaged belt. HMG offers replacements in three different sizes to fit a wider variety of bodies.

Bottom pocket: There is a low-profile pocket on the bottom of the Unbound, made from the same Dyneema stretch mesh as the front mesh pocket. One side is open for easy, pack-on access, while a smaller opening at one corner is ideal for stuffing snack wrappers.

Roll top: This classic design helps seal out the elements and adjust pack volume as it fluctuates during a hike. The extension collar expands for those big food hauls, but rolls down for tidier loads.

Y top strap: Far from unique, yet functional. The adjustable Y strap clips over the top of the pack and is useful for securing bulky items such as a foam sleeping pad, bear canister, or bag of chips.

Adjustable compression: Two daisy chains running vertically in parallel up each side of the Unbound provide numerous attachment points for compression straps and/or straps for lashing bulky items. The included shock cord zig-zags help secure tall items in the side pockets — umbrella, tent poles, trekking poles, etc.

Two frame stays instead of one. That’s an improvement.

The updated Unbound is essentially the same as the original. However, three significant changes distinguish the new from the old. HMG made some other tweaks as well, like different sewing, a new color, and pack shape, and I apologize to the design team for glossing over them, but here are the big ones:

New Dyneema Fabric: In a world where folks get excited about new shoe colors every six months, HMG’s commitment to its classic DCH fabric has been remarkable. During the decade spanning my first thru-hike in 2015 until last year, not much changed with the brand’s famous white backpacks. They remained popular throughout that period, even without jumping on the trends that swept through the UL cottage industry.

However, in 2025, HMG redesigned the Southwest, Windrider, and Junction with a new fabric. Now the Unbound benefits from the same upgrade.

This new Dyneema Woven Composite (DWC) promises improved abrasion resistance, strength, durability, and flexibility, while weighing less than the outgoing DCH. This is achieved partly through 100% Dyneema construction, whereas the old DCH incorporated some polyester. Time will tell if it is a true improvement, but all the data and my experience back up the claims.

More Frame Stays: The original Unbound utilized a single frame stay. The new Unbound has two. Theoretically, this equals greater load-carrying capacity, although the maximum recommended load is still 40 pounds. I was satisfied with the old frame, but I’m happy to have the extra structure.

New shoulder straps: The updated straps are wider and more curved than HMG’s old design — more of an ’S’ shape than before. This promises more comfort for a wider range of body types by distributing the pack load more evenly.

The big side pockets carried a lot of weird stuff on this trip, and it all stayed secure, despite the scrambling. Photo cred: aeigenbrot

As I hoped and expected, the team at Hyperlite has improved the Unbound in meaningful ways without any compromises that I’m aware of. Everything I loved about the original backpack is still there, but with the new fabric, shoulder straps, and stouter frame, the second generation is more comfortable, durable, and capable. It’s also lighter. Who said there’s no free lunch?

That being said, backpacks are highly personal. I love the simplicity of the Unbound and similar packs, but if you want more than the most basic features, then you might want to keep looking. The Unbound is designed to excel at a specific purpose.

Without casting shade on the fancy new fabric (which I do like a lot), the excellent side pockets have been and remain the Unbound’s defining feature. I loved them on the original Unbound, and I love them now.

Many other perfectly good backpacks fall short with side pockets that are either too small, difficult to reach, or insecure (including other HMG packs). I hate struggling to squeeze a bottle into an unforgivingly tight pocket, and I think it’s ridiculous that some are only large enough to fit narrow Smartwater bottles. What about my pot? What about my large-capacity water bottle? How about my tent poles, fuel canister, or umbrella? Just give me space to jam all my weirdly sized crap, dammit.

The Unbound does give me that space. My weirdly sized crap jams in nicely, and a cinch cord keeps everything secure. All side pockets should be exactly like these.

The Unbound is as comfortable as any other lightweight pack that I’ve owned. It may not have trampoline-like suspension, but it holds loads well and resists barreling. The foam back panel also did a good job of protecting my back from weird points and metal climbing hardware when I got lazy with my packing.

While more cushioned shoulder straps and hip belts exist, the Unbound’s were adequately supportive, even when my pack hovered around 40 pounds. I can’t say that the new wider and differently shaped shoulder straps were obviously better, but they did the job without drawing attention.

Finally, the new DWC fabric is nicer to the touch than DCH. It’s more flexible and less crinkly, although no one will mistake it for velvet.

The bottom of the Unbound is DWC, putting durability where it’s needed most. The fabric is mostly fine after a punishing trip, but there are some scuffed fibers.

Combining my experience carrying the new Unbound, the old Unbound, and other Hyperlite backpacks, I’m confident that durability is not an issue. I never had any problems with abrasion or punctures in the past, and the new DWC fabric has survived plenty of abuse.

During my trip to the Winds, I banged and scraped the bottom of the side pockets across countless granite boulders without springing a leak. While the upper portion of each pocket is made from Dyneema Gridstop, the DWC is wisely deployed on the bottom where it is most needed. If not for this, I’m certain that there would be several holes.

In short, I was impressed with DWC, and putting it on the bottom of the pockets was a hero move by HMG. The Unbound should easily survive most thru-hikes and high routes.

The volume difference comes from the extension collar. Seen here, unrolled above the shoulders.

The Unbound is available in both 40- and 55-liter volumes. I typically prefer 40-liter backpacks. The smaller capacity keeps me honest. However, I opted for the 55-liter version this time for greater versatility above and beyond what I might need for a three-season thru-hike (i.e., winter trips, mountaineering, SAR, etc.).

Regardless of the pack volume, the dimensions are the same except for the height and top width of the extension collar. In other words, the 55L Unbound is the same as the 40L version, but with a taller roll-top. This extra fabric is easy to roll away and adds roughly 1.5 ounces to the total weight.

The way I see it, the extra weight is a fair price to pay for extra versatility, and it’s already come in handy on a few occasions. As always, I recommend considering your own needs honestly based on the expected usage and the rest of your gear list.

Both the Unbound 40L and 50L can handle your standard ~11-liter bear canister like most other backpacks in this category. Pack it inside vertically, or use the long Y-strap to secure it outside, on top.

While I don’t have anything to complain about, I would love to try a version of the Unbound that incorporates running-vest-style shoulder straps with even more pockets for snacks and smaller items. I believe this would align with the intended use quite well by keeping high-use essentials within reach, enabling longer distances between breaks. Sure, you can add shoulder pockets or wear a fanny pack, but running-vest utility is undeniable.

DWC kept the contents of my Unbound dry during a morning of rain. I still used a pack liner, though. Photo cred: aeigenbrot

Side pockets: Goodness gracious, all side pockets should be like the ones on the Unbound. Dare I say that they’re perfect? They hold a ton of stuff, yet remain secure and easy to access while wearing the pack for folks with average shoulder mobility. And having DWC on the bottom of each pocket puts durability exactly where it is needed most. Seriously, the Unbound’s side pockets have spoiled me. I now begrudge packs that I used to love because of their inferior pockets.

Removable hip belt: All bodies are different, and all bodies change, especially over the course of a long thru-hike. It might not be convenient to purchase a new hip belt, but at least the option is there. It’s also nice to ditch the hip belt sometimes without taking scissors to your fancy backpack.

Dyneema: The new DWC is a great backpack material. It’s plenty durable for most adventures yet is lightweight and weather resistant. Dyneema is a cornerstone of all HMG designs, and it’s hard to argue with that choice.

Updates: The new Unbound’s updates make a pack that I already loved even better. The fabric is tougher, lighter, and more flexible. The extra frame stay boosts carrying comfort. The new shoulder straps should fit more people comfortably. The old, single-stay frame was maybe the one reason to choose HMG’s other packs instead of the Unbound. That’s not a thing anymore.

Lightweight: Even with the extra frame stay, the Unbound is lighter than before. And it is still the lightest of HMG’s popular thru-hiking backpacks. There are backpacks out there that weigh less, don’t get me wrong, but the Unbound is a lot of backpack for under two pounds.

Dyneema fabric is awesome, but it is pricey.

No load lifters: HMG has never included these useful straps on their core backpacks, but that doesn’t mean I don’t miss them. Running from the top of the shoulder straps to the top of the back panel, they can lift some of the load off the shoulders and also adjust how close the pack conforms to the back. It’s possible that the Unbound’s frame isn’t compatible with this system, and that by keeping weight low you don’t need them. However, if you want load lifters, look elsewhere.

No hydration port: If you prefer drinking from a straw instead of bottles, then you’re out of luck. I could not find a hydration port on the Unbound, even though some of HMG’s other backpacks do have one.

Expensive: While the Unbound is priced similarly to other Dyneema backpacks, it still costs significantly more than non-Dyneema peers. For the upcharge, you get better durability for a similar weight and more weather resistance. A fair tradeoff, but the Unbound is not a budget pick.

Without knowing what lies ahead, it’s nice to be confident that the Unbound can handle it. Photo cred: aeigenbrot

The updated HMG Unbound is easily my favorite backpack. If I truly need to haul a gargantuan load, then I’ll reach for something with a more substantial frame. However, for all other applications, the Unbound has me covered.

Where the new version rises above the old is in the new DWC material and double-stay frame. These are significant improvements that increase durability and functionality while reducing weight. They also bring the Unbound up to spec with HMG’s flagship thru-hiking backpacks, which were first in line for the DWC upgrade and always had two frame stays.

Honestly, I now cannot conjure a coherent argument in favor of the Southwest, Windrider, or Junction over the Unbound. Maybe I’m missing something, so please let me know if you disagree.

Regardless, the Unbound is worth considering if you’re in the market for a new backpack. It’s simple yet functional. It’s lightweight and durable. Whether it’s a high route, long trail, or weekend getaway, this pack is ready to go wherever you want to carry it.

A Night in a Camino Ghost Town

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The streets of Arzúa were completely deserted again as we set out this morning. It felt almost apocalyptic—a good-sized, modern city with well-kept buildings that were clearly occupied, yet not a single person was in sight. I kept expecting zombies to come out of an alleyway.

On our way out of town, we ran into our tramily and ended up hiking together for the rest of the day. Good conversation flowed easily, making the miles fly by. Surprisingly, the terrain kept us on our toes with several sharp climbs and steep descents as we passed through a handful of ancient hamlets.

The Camino was busy with walkers again today, giving the trek more of a touristy atmosphere than a quiet pilgrimage. Still, it was a pleasant hike overall. Most of the route guided us through lush woodland paths, past sprawling farm fields, and along sections framed by towering stone walls.

As we crossed an interstate highway via an overpass bridge, a light rain began to fall. It was our first rain in weeks, but it was brief and refreshing, keeping temperatures cool in the mid-60s. The chain-link fence lining the bridge was covered in thousands of wooden crosses, notes, and memorials left behind by passing pilgrims.

The remaining miles melted away as we entered our final destination for the day: O Pedrouzo (also known as Arca). Like Arzúa, O Pedrouzo is a modern town built primarily to service the influx of pilgrims on the final stretch to Santiago, so there wasn’t a whole lot of sightseeing to do.

For dinner, we stumbled upon a great Mexican restaurant where I ordered seafood paella. The dish was cooked with squid ink (paella negra), which turned the rice jet black while imparting a rich, savory flavor. Knockerz opted for an authentic Mexican burrito, which hit the spot and tasted just like the ones back home.

After dinner, we relaxed on the small patio outside our albergue room. We spent the evening sipping wine and soaking in sweeping views of the valley, forests, and distant mountains—knowing tomorrow would be our final push into Santiago de Compostela.

Polestar unveils Formula 2030 concept, previewing the next Polestar 2

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Polestar has now fully unveiled the Formula 2030 concept, a low four-door electric sports car that previews the design of the next-generation Polestar 2 coming in 2027 and the Polestar 7 compact SUV.

The concept sits just 1,350 mm (53 inches) tall, which is lower than the Polestar 5 grand tourer, and it brings physical buttons back into the Swedish brand’s interior.

We reported in September that Polestar confirmed the Formula 2030 concept after showing it privately to investors and retailers in Gothenburg. Now we get the full set of pictures and the details.

It’s the first show car built under head of design Philipp Römers, who joined from Audi in 2024.

Polestar calls the Formula 2030 a “compact practical four-door sports car” that “sits within its own segment.” It’s 4,680 mm (184.3 inches) long and 2,021 mm (79.6 inches) wide, on a 2,780 mm (109.4-inch) wheelbase.

For context, that’s about 3 inches longer than today’s Polestar 2 and roughly 5 inches lower. It’s also a bit shorter than a Tesla Model 3 and sits 3.5 inches closer to the ground. Polestar says the concept lands “somewhere between” the upcoming Polestar 2 and Polestar 7 in size.

The proportions are classic sports car: long hood, short overhangs, the cabin pushed back over big rear haunches, and 22-inch center-lock wheels. There are gold Brembo calipers behind those wheels and Öhlins dampers on display in the trunk.

Up front, Polestar kept its “Dual Blade” headlights, now set into an aero wing over a shark nose and a deep splitter. The automaker says this is the face of the next-generation Polestar 2 planned for 2027.

There’s a single vertical windshield wiper, like on a race car, and a LiDAR unit that hides under an aerodynamic shroud and only shows itself when the car is driving autonomously.

The rear is where things change the most. Polestar dropped the full-width light bar, which is on just about every EV these days, for what it calls “Single Blade” rear lights. It says they are being introduced here “ahead of series production.”

Römers described the concept as a “bold evolution” of the brand’s existing design:

“It is a physical embodiment of how we apply Polestar’s core values of design, performance and sustainability to the next production cars.”

The rear doors are hinged at the back and there’s no B-pillar, so the whole side opens up to four individual seats.

The interior is built around the driver. A curved 20-inch display, which Polestar calls the “HyperDIM,” is mounted directly on the steering column, and a 12.2-inch center screen sits in the center tunnel.

But the bigger news is below that screen. Polestar added a panel of eight physical controls for the most-used functions, and owners could swap between types of controls, like rocker switches or rotary knobs, and program what each one does.

Polestar says it is “committing to rebalance the mix of physical and digital.” Juan Pablo Bernal, the company’s head of interior design, put it plainly:

“As a performance brand, we see a renewed focus on the driver, and so we re-balance the digital and physical. We introduce physical buttons and controls.”

That’s a big change for a brand whose recent cars run almost everything through the center touchscreen.

The cabin also physically changes with the drive mode. There are three of them: Performance, Normal, and Autonomous. In Performance, the center screen drops flush into the console and the steering wheel and its display pull closer to the driver. In Autonomous, the wheel and the seat move away from each other.

The four seats use inflatable bolsters that shape themselves to each occupant, and the seat backs are made of a flax-based composite instead of virgin plastic. Google Gemini is built in, like in Polestar’s production cars.

The cargo area is the strangest part of the car. The rear opens as a clamshell, and the trunk floor is transparent, with custom Polestar bags strapped down underneath it with gold straps.

Up front, the frunk holds two race helmets and a fire extinguisher, framed by a gold strut brace.

Now for what’s missing. Polestar didn’t release a single powertrain spec. No battery size, no range, no power output, and no price. This is a design study, and Polestar isn’t saying it will build it.

What it does say is that “various design attributes” from the concept will show up on the next Polestar 2 in 2027 and the Polestar 7 in 2028. Those are two of the four new models Polestar plans to launch in three years, and the 7 is going to be built in Europe.

Europe is where this matters, since Polestar is shut out of the US under the federal connected-vehicle rule. The concept made its debut during the media drive of the Polestar 4 SUV.

Polestar always impresses me with its design, and this is no exception with this new head of design.

The proportions are sharp. Long hood, cabin pushed back, big haunches, and a roofline lower than the Polestar 5’s on a car with four doors and four real seats. If the next Polestar 2 keeps even half of this stance, it’s going to be the best-looking car in its segment.

I don’t know about the trunk of this thing. Polestar itself calls the rear “UFO-esque,” and between the wraparound glass, the clamshell and the see-through floor, it’s a lot. But everything else on this car is pretty sharp.

Polestar is putting physical controls back in its cars and saying so in plain words. The way they talk about it makes it sound like you can expect that of upcoming production vehicles.

Now the usual caveat. A concept car doesn’t fix a balance sheet. Polestar lost $842 million in the first half of this year on a negative gross margin. Design has never been Polestar’s problem. Building these cars and selling them at a profit is.

After Wasserman’s Exit, The Team Forms New Board of Directors

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The 11-member board is comprised of leaders across the Mike Watts-led sports, music and representation giant as the rebranded firm charts its next chapter under Providence Equity Partners.

As it looks to chart a path away from its namesake founder, the Hollywood representation powerhouse The Team is installing a new board of directors stocked with leaders from key constituencies across the company.

The Westwood-based firm, formerly known as Wasserman, unveiled an 11-person board on Tuesday comprised of in-house exec members. Mike Watts, the CEO who took the helm amid founder Casey Wasserman’s tumultuous exit tied to an artist exodus in February, will serve on the board.

So too will Jon Liebman, the chief of Brillstein Entertainment Partners, the pioneering management and production firm that was acquired by Wasserman three years ago in a major expansion.

Kevin Shivers, who was promoted in April to co-president of music as part of the stabilization of that department (which had been bulked up through the acquisition of Paradigm’s roster five years ago), is also taking a seat on the board.

Leaders like Elizabeth Lindsey, who oversees the brands and properties business, Jason Ranne, who leads talent representation, the U.K.-based international chief Matthew Vandrau and CFO Elena Rochelli were also named to the board, as were C-suiters Davis Noell, Greg Brown and Scott Marimow.

Providence Equity Partners, the majority investor in The Team which formally bought out Wasserman’s interest after a bidding process over the summer, added its SVP Ahana Maken to the board and says that it will “name additional external board members in the coming months.” The firm has been invested in the company since 2022 when it took the place of prior backers RedBird Capital and Madrone Capital Partners.

The Team is one of the biggest players in the fast-growing sports representation space, along with CAA. And it has a large music roster that is being rebuilt after a slew of departures made waves in that space when Wasserman was linked to Ghislaine Maxwell over decades-old flirtatious emails unearthed by the Department of Justice in January.

The company, originally founded in 2002, has snapped up many multiple boutique agencies and had 4,000 employees as of earlier this year. Its latest acquisition was unveiled just last month, when it bought Outreach Talent Group, co-founded by Emilio Arciniega, which has its main base in London.

Today, only legends surround the crystals of Irm. The red crystal of the mountains – once the fire of the great forge, the blue crystal – once the symbol of the knowledge of the eternal academy and the green crystal – once the patron saint of the endless forests; they have all disappeared from the world for too long. Inflation Remains Hot in October and Strengthens Case for Another Fed Rate Hike

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Inflation accelerated faster than anticipated in October, driven by surging costs for housing and food, which will bolster the case for another Federal Reserve interest rate hike next month.

On a monthly basis, overall prices rose 0.4%, according to the Consumer Price Index (CPI) data released released by the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) on Tuesday. That's down from the 0.6% monthly increase recorded in September but higher than the 0.2% gain forecasted by economists.

CPI measures the average costs consumers pay for a range of goods, including food, energy, commodities, and services.

Core inflation, which strips out volatile food and energy categories, rose 0.3%, also exceeding the anticipated 0.2% increase.

The BLS did not publish an official annual inflation rate because last year's government shutdown prevented the agency from collecting survey data for October 2025.

Michael Carrick takes training as Man Utd hierarchy ponders his future

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Carrick’s future is still up in the air with Mauricio Pochettino a top contender to replace him

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Michael Carrick took training on Tuesday as Manchester United’s powerbrokers drag their heels over the beleaguered head coach’s future ahead of a key meeting in Monaco.

The former midfielder’s days in charge appear to be numbered after overseeing the club’s worst start to a campaign since 1986, leaving the Red Devils in the relegation zone over the international break.

United, who languish in 18th, are using the two-week hiatus to review Carrick’s position and consider alternatives, with the head coach widely expected to lose his job following discussions.

But Old Trafford sources remained adamant on Tuesday lunchtime that no decision had been made after the 45-year-old reported to the Carrington training complex earlier that day to take training.

Carrick’s future is set to be decided 800 miles away from Manchester in Monaco, where director of football Jason Wilcox and chief executive Omar Berrada are due to give their views to United’s ownership.

A meeting is reportedly scheduled in the principality on Wednesday with co-owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe, whose Ineos company controls football operations and would sign off a change at the top with the Glazers.

United are keeping tight-lipped on the process and relevant meetings, which is only prolonging the agony for popular former midfielder Carrick, who made 464 appearances for the club between 2006 and 2018.

Mauricio Pochettino, the former Tottenham, Paris Saint-Germain and Chelsea boss, has emerged as the leading contender to replace him.

The former Argentina international has held talks with United in the past and is understood to be interested in the Old Trafford post, but hefty compensation would be due for the United States boss.

Just three months ago Pochettino and his staff extended their deals until 2030 and are currently in Georgia preparing for Nations League quarter-final fixtures against Haiti on Saturday and Tuesday.

Aston Villa boss Unai Emery is admired, while ex-Newcastle boss Eddie Howe and former Germany head coach Julian Nagelsmann were linked a fortnight ago when United seriously considered replacing Carrick.

A 4-1 Champions League loss at Como and 3-0 home defeat to Bournemouth left the head coach’s job hanging by a thread, but the club decided to give him the opportunity to turn the situation around.

Carrick’s side subsequently lost 2-0 at Chelsea, where they were unable to take their chances and saw Lisandro Martinez sent off, before last Tuesday’s thrilling 3-2 Champions League comeback win against Roma.

A scarcely believable 4-4 draw against Aston Villa followed on Sunday, after which the head coach stressed he would not walk away and was looking to the international break as usual.

“It’s been a right mixed week or two or three, really, if you’re looking at the events,” Carrick said. “But I’m pretty clear where we need to go and how we need to get there.

“Obviously some of the boys will be flying off around the world and different things, and the group will be split for 10 days or so.

“But as a normal international week is, you know, we’ll have some training. We’ll have one or two days off, but not too much. But we’ll train, and we’ll get ready for the next game.”

United return to action with a Premier League trip to rivals Liverpool on 22 November.

London Stadium to host big boxing events and increase concerts as it seeks to reduce £20m taxpayer bill

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Get our award-winning daily news email featuring exclusive stories, opinion and expert analysis

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

The London Stadium is set to host large-scale boxing matches and increase concerts as the Greater London Authority seeks to reduce the £20 million yearly bill taxpayers face from its operation.

West Ham United’s ground owners want to challenge Wembley as the capital’s premier location for events in a bid to raise much-needed revenue.

The GLA owns the London Stadium LLP and the football team plays there under an agreement running until 2115.

Taxpayers have shouldered the £19 million yearly bill for the operating loss of the stadium.

When West Ham were relegated from the Premier League last season, the £4.6 million annual rent was slashed by £2.5 million under terms set out in the club’s contract, pushing this further cost onto Londoners.

Commercial revenues from the former Olympic stadium have also fallen and the GLA will incur higher stewarding costs because there are 23 home games in the Championship compared with 19 in the Premier League.

Under plans going before Newham council on Monday, the stadium authority is seeking permission to increase annual events from 10 to 24.

Boxing and Soccer Aid will be recategorised under the plans so crowds of 85,000 people can attend, up from current restrictions of 60,000.

Maximum capacity for eight events a year will rise to 85,000, while the others will stay at the current capacity of 80,000.

Consecutive nights for concerts will also increase from three to four, under the proposals.

Last summer, the stadium hosted Take That over three nights and Metallica for two in a bid to boost revenue.

But the extra income only just covered the losses incurred by West Ham’s relegation and rent reduction.

Under the stadium authority’s proposals, capacity for all sports events will increase to 62,500 to match the capacity of West Ham games.

West Ham produce stunning three-goal comeback in Parker's first game in charge

Rediscover your adventurous side on an exotic getaway

It is hoped that staging more boxing matches at the ground will be a huge cash raiser.

An upcoming bout between Anthony Joshua and Tyson Fury at Cardiff’s 80,000-seater Principality Stadium in December is expected to bring in excess of £20 million.

It comes after Mayor Sadiq Khan was urged earlier this year to sell the London Stadium to West Ham.

Labour London Assembly member Bassam Mahfouz urged Lord Khan to consider selling the stadium to the football club as way to avoid losing tens of millions of pounds a year.

He said the current arrangement, where West Ham pay rent to use the stadium as their home ground, had proved “disastrous” for London taxpayers.

Over the summer, Lyn Garner, Chair of London Stadium LLP, warned that “the financial situation remains challenging”.

“In 25/26, we are going to finalise the accounts at around a £19m operating loss,” Ms Garner told a meeting of City Hall’s Economy, Culture and Skills Committee.

“This is not unusual. It's been in this ballpark for some time. We have a few peaks and troughs, depending on how many summer events we have.”

She added that 25/26 had been a “particularly bad year for London Stadium, with only two summer concerts”.

Chris Pratt Lost Chuck To Zachary Levi. Years Later, Jurassic World Gave Him A Full-Circle Moment

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Andrew Garfield Just Had A Really Morbid Take On Playing Spider-Man (But He’s Not Wrong)

As Matt Damon Comes Off His Big Odyssey Year, He’s Getting Real About The Low Points Of His Career

Chris Pratt Dropped A Spider-Man Meme To Support Amazon’s Action Stars. Alan Ritchson’s Reaction Is A+

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Being a struggling actor in Hollywood is tough, and even huge stars like Chris Pratt go through the wringer early in their careers (like working at Bubba Gump Shrimp). Such was the case when Pratt lost out on the titular role on the show Chuck. Pratt auditioned for the role in 2007, but lost it to Zachary Levi. That has got to be hard enough, but for Pratt, it was made worse as he was taunted by a billboard for the show in LA. Pratt got the last laugh on the casting directors of the show years later when he got his own poster on the same building for Jurassic World.

After trying out for and not making the final cut for Chuck, things got worse when a billboard Pratt could see from his apartment went up advertising the show, with Levi featured on it. Pratt recently talked about it with Barstool:

I did a screen test for Chuck. I wanted it so bad, and then there was this giant billboard across from my apartment of Zach Levi as Chuck, and I’m like, ‘grrr.’

Imagine waking up every morning and looking out the window at a billboard that you hoped would have your face on it, only to see someone else you lost out to. It would be maddening! Pratt kept at it, of course, and eventually landed gigs in Jennifer’s Body and Moneyball, before really breaking big as part of the ensemble cast of Parks and Rec. It wasn’t until 2015 when the moment came full circle, at the premiere of one of Pratt’s earliest blockbusters, Jurassic World.

The moment wasn’t lost on Pratt, as he explained:

Then that same building is where we had the premiere for Jurassic World. And that same building had a giant poster on it with me on a motorcycle, and I was like, 'whoa!'

That really is coming full circle, from what sounds like a real low point early in his career to the triumph of starring in one of the biggest franchises in Hollywood history. Jurassic World came right on the heels of Pratt joining the MCU as Star-Lord in the first Guardians of the Galaxy, so he had become one of the biggest movie stars on the planet.

So does this mean he’s got a “hidden rivalry” with Levi? Well, probably not. He doesn’t seem to have an animosity towards the Shazam! star, just frustration for not getting a part at that point in his career. It’s safe to say that things have worked out pretty well for Pratt, who will next be seen on the 2026 movie schedule in Way of the Warrior Kid, which will be available to watch with an Apple TV subscription.

Tiny Solar Whirlpools May Hold the Secret to the Sun’s Blistering Corona

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

For the second time in a few weeks, weâre writing about research into the temperature gap between the Sunâs surface and its corona. More and more data from various missions continue to feed this wave of discovery. A new paper by Katariina Nykyri of Embry-Riddle, recently published in The Astrophysical Journal Letters, adds to this growing body of work. She argues that millions of tiny whirlpools scattered across the Sunâs surface could be the energetic engine driving that temperature difference.

Earlier this year, the Daniel K. Inouye Solar Telescope (DKIST), located atop HaleakalÄ on Maui, took extremely high resolution images of the Sunâs surface. For the first time, scientists could make out whirlpools of plasma, a few dozen kilometers across, dotted around the Sunâs surface. When this research was published in August, Dr. Nykyri had a bit of a âEureka!â moment, according to a press release from the University.

Her research had focused on Kelvin-Helmholtz waves. On Earth, these are most commonly seen in broken ocean swells or ripple-pattern clouds. However, they are also present elsewhere in the solar system, driving gas patterns on Jupiter. On the Sun, they occur when fast-moving plasma passes slower, magnetized plasma. They contain an absurd amount of mechanical energy. The vortices created by these waves are the very structures DKIST resolved on our starâs surface. As these vortices churn across the solar surface, their vast kinetic energy transfers into the Sun's magnetic fields, converting mechanical motion into magnetic stress.

Fraser talks about the features of the Sun that we are only now getting a glimpse of thanks to new technologies.

Dr. Nykyri set out to answer how much energy these vortices carry and whether it equals the total energy needed to heat the corona. After accounting for the velocity shear, density difference, and vertical heights of the tornadoes, she reached a conclusion. On average, each vortex contains around two septillion ergsâa unit of energy commonly used in solar physics. Across the Sunâs 1.4-million-kilometer surface, millions of these vortices spin up and dissipate every few minutes.

When these vortices dissipate, their energy must go somewhere. According to Dr. Nykyri, it appears to enter the corona through magnetic reconnection. Solar physicists have long thought this process plays a key role in coronal heating. As each KH vortex spins, it squeezes nearby magnetic field lines together. This forms ultra-thin current lines ranging from a few centimeters to meters across.

Once theyâre squeezed past a certain point, they start to violently snap and rearrange. That is the process of magnetic reconnection, and it converts the stored magnetic energy into heat and high-speed plasma jets that are fundamental components of the solar corona. In other words, the KH vortices are the driving force behind the process of reconnection of the Sunâs magnetic field lines.

Frase talks about how hot the Sun actually is.

When the energy they store is released, it has to have somewhere to go, though. And these vortices donât always cause reconnections only in the corona - they also do so on the surface. So why does the corona seem so much hotter? Particle density.

When a magnetic line snaps on the surface, the energy is immediately distributed between trillions of neutral atoms. Each atomâs temperature is commensurately raised, but only by a tiny amount. In the corona, though, atoms are much less plentiful, but the same amount of energy is released. So each magnetic snap increases the temperature of an individual atom by much more in the corona than down on the surface.

According to the paper, only five to eight percent of the mechanical energy stored in the vortices needs to make the journey upward to the corona to replenish the heat that is otherwise radiated away. Even in darker âcoronal holesâ, a max of 21% of the energy from the vortices would be capable of maintaining the temperature of the corona.

This work is a major step toward understanding the heating process, but more research is needed. Scientists still must track wave transmission and polarization through the chromosphere. This will confirm whether as much energy reaches the Sunâs chromosphere as Dr. Nykyri suggests is necessary to maintain coronal temperatures. If her theory holds, some of the high temperatures on our star will be proven to stem from relatively small plasma tornadoes floating across its surface.

Anthropic targets $1.4 trillion IPO

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Anthropic confirmed on Tuesday that its IPO could be one of the biggest ever, even while falling short of previous expectations. Its IPO price is expected to be between $70.00 and $78.00 per share and according to the Wall Street Journal’s sources, Anthropic is “aiming for its shares to start trading Thursday or Friday next week.”

As Reuters notes, the Anthropic IPO was previously expected to be valued at as much as $2 trillion. As of last week, Anthropic’s rival OpenAI was valued at $1.25 trillion.

The initial public offering price is expected to be between $70.00 and $78.00 per share. Anthropic has applied to list its Class A common stock on the Nasdaq Global Select Market under the symbol “ANT.”
