2027 Chevrolet Silverado HD, a Gorilla-Strong Pickup You Don’t Have to Wrestle With

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A monstrously torquey new 8.3-liter turbo-diesel V-8 and new electric power steering make this heavy-duty pickup easy to drive.

The lightest Atlas stone used in international strongman competitions is 220 pounds. The heaviest is nearly 500 pounds. Extremely large, frighteningly strong people lift these big orbs onto shoulder-high plinths, and just watching it will give you an inferiority complex—and sciatica. Luckily, there’s a way to feel a similar sense of broad-shouldered accomplishment without spending a lifetime in the gym. Drive the 2027 Chevy Silverado Heavy-Duty (HD) pickup, preferably with a trailer attached, and you’ll get the same rush of endorphins as 2026 World Strongest Man champion Mitchell Hooper as he lifted spheres and repped deadlifts of over 800 pounds.

While the 2027 Silverado 1500 got a full redesign for 2027, the HD is mostly carryover in body and interior. Still, it has some new delights, the biggest and most delightful being the new 8.3-liter Duramax V-8 turbo-diesel engine. This big brute belts out 555 horsepower and 1230 pound-feet of torque, is backed by a 10-speed automatic transmission, and makes light work of towing even the heaviest trailer.

See all results for new 2027 Chevrolet Silverado 2500HD / 3500HD for sale near 95131

To keep the burly engine happy, the HD trucks have a revised cooling system with a four-deep stack of radiators, charge coolers, and oil coolers. To keep the driver cool when stopping a fully loaded HD pickup, 14.6-inch front brake rotors clamped by four-piston calipers replace the outgoing model’s two-piston binders. An electronic brake booster, a first for a heavy-duty pickup, gives the brake pedal a similar feel to what you’d find in a regular SUV, no heavy foot needed for effective braking.

To demonstrate the diesel’s grunt, Chevy hooked up a massive trailer loaded with construction equipment and let us rip, towing nearly 37,000 pounds down a drag strip. The new trucks are rated for up to 38,000 pounds with a gooseneck trailer (when equipped with the Max Trailering package). The NHRA won’t be adding a sled-pulling class to its competition anytime soon, but the big truck moved out with barely a shudder, and the upgraded brakes and e-booster made stopping at the far end as easy as launching. The most intimidating part of the pull was making the turnout at the end of the strip, but the HD’s smooth power delivery and redesigned steering made for a drama-free turn despite the tight corner.

The 2500 HD lineup encompasses six trims, starting with the WT (Work Truck) and topping out with the optioned-up High Country. The base engine remains a 6.6-liter gas-burning V-8 (distinct from the similarly sized optional V-8 in redesigned 2027 light-duty models), albeit updated to now develop 412 horsepower and 472 pound-feet of torque. The new Duramax V-8 is an $11,400 option, and it’s available on all trims. That’s a big jump from the outgoing 6.6-liter diesel V-8, which added only $9990 to the HD’s price. Luckily for truck shoppers, overall starting prices see a smaller price hike. The MSRP for the 2027 WT begins at $49,995 (an increase of $1000 versus 2026), and the High Country now starts at $78,195.

See all results for new 2027 Chevrolet Silverado 2500HD / 3500HD for sale near 95131

Step up into the cabin—or haul yourself up with the grab handle—and you’ll find the interior is little changed from 2026 models. It’s spacious and cushy with big storage compartments, but the design and the dash layout are the same as before. What is new is some of the software available through the 13.4-inch center touchscreen. There’s an updated Tow/Haul mode that fine-tunes power delivery, shift points, and braking to make for smoother towing up and downhill. An Off-Road mode allows more wheelspin to prevent bog-downs, and off-road-oriented ZR2 HDs get the same Terrain mode as the light-duty version, a trail-focused tune that teams the throttle with automatic braking for what’s essentially one-pedal driving during off-roading. Optional camera add-ons make for up to 14 different views, including an invisible-trailer mode and a split screen when reversing. In the various camera views, the HD lacks the light duty’s helpful graphic overlays, but it’s probably fair to assume that buyers of heavy-duty trucks aren’t beginners at towing.

Another feature that has not made its way from light duty to heavy duty is Chevy’s Super Cruise hands-free driving. In the past, this was because the HD still used a hydraulic steering system, but a major change for 2027 is the switch to electric power steering. The new rack-and-pinion electric steering setup is another first for the HD, and it makes a big difference in the feedback through the wheel and consistency of steering feel even during braking and hard steering inputs. It gives the HD a more consumer-friendly feel. On a mountain road, these big rigs feel manageable, and while you still won’t want to swing a tight U-turn, there’s no fighting the wheel when changing direction. While this year’s trucks won’t offer Super Cruise, the technology is in place for GM to fit additional driver aids in the future.

That omission aside, the theme of the HDs’ changes for 2027 is ease of use. Most of what’s new isn’t visible from the outside, but it’s all designed to make the tasks of hauling and towing feel less like a job and more like a cruise in a passenger vehicle—a really burly passenger vehicle.

Lufthansa Suddenly Cancels This 13-Hour Nonstop Route: See Map Now

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Last month, Lufthansa announced its most extensive route expansion since 2020. For summer 2027, the Star Alliance member is adding ten new long-haul routes across Asia and the Americas, as well as increasing frequencies on several existing services.

While not part of the announcement, the German flag carrier was also scheduled to resume nonstop flights between Munich Airport (MUC) and Hong Kong International Airport (HKG) after several years of absence. However, in its latest weekend schedule update, the airline removed the route altogether.

Lufthansa has been connecting Germany and Hong Kong since 1961, initially operating flights from Frankfurt Airport (FRA). In the 2000s, the airline also introduced flights from Munich. The route continued operating until early 2020 and has remained suspended since then. The German flag carrier was scheduled to resume flights between Munich and Hong Kong on March 28, 2027, using the Airbus A350-900.

This was also the aircraft type used on the route before its suspension in 2020. In fact, over the years, the airline has operated multiple widebody aircraft on the route, including the Airbus A340-300 and A340-600. It also deployed its Airbus A380 superjumbo in 2018 and 2019.

Why Lufthansa has decided not to reinstate the Munich–Hong Kong route remains unknown. Nevertheless, Cathay Pacific, which is Hong Kong's largest airline, will continue to connect the two cities. The airline launched four weekly flights between Munich and Hong Kong in June last year, operating on Mondays, Tuesdays, Thursdays and Saturdays, using its A350-900 aircraft.

Lufthansa, meanwhile, will continue to serve Hong Kong from its Frankfurt hub, using the Boeing 787-9 Dreamliner. According to schedule data from aviation analytics provider Cirium, the airline is scheduled to operate one daily round-trip flight on the route throughout the year. Cathay Pacific also offers daily flights between Frankfurt and Hong Kong.

This means there will be a total of 18 weekly services from Germany to Hong Kong next summer. Looking more broadly, the German airline is scheduled to operate 76 weekly flights across 11 East Asian routes in summer 2027. These include the return of services to Nagoya and Tokyo Narita in Japan, both from its Frankfurt hub.

From March 29, 2027, the Star Alliance member will operate three weekly flights to Chubu Centrair International Airport (NGO), which it last served in 2020. A day earlier, on March 28, it will also resume daily flights to Tokyo Narita Airport (NRT). The airline last operated passenger flights to Narita in 2017, when it consolidated its Tokyo operations at Haneda Airport (HND).

In general, Lufthansa has been expanding its network from both Frankfurt and Munich. The airline introduced several new destinations this winter and has more routes planned for summer 2027. From its Southern hub, it will launch three weekly flights to Phoenix Sky Harbor International Airport (PHX) on August 3, operating on Tuesdays, Fridays and Sundays with the A350-900. It will also increase frequencies to Bangalore, Chicago, São Paulo and Singapore.

It would not be surprising to see the German flag carrier adding more routes from Munich in the future, especially given its plans to expand its long-haul operations at the airport. It has extended its joint venture agreement with Munich Airport until 2056. Under the agreement, both parties plan to expand terminal infrastructure and support the growth of Lufthansa's long-haul fleet and operations at the airport.

Munich is already home to the airline's A380 fleet and remains its main hub for the A350-900. According to Cirium schedule data, the airline has scheduled about 6,341 flights using the A350-900 from Munich between January and October 2027, compared to 1,492 from Frankfurt. This is an increase of 7% at Munich and 70% at Frankfurt compared to the same period in 2026.

2027 Chevrolet Silverado HD

Photoshop Now Relights and Restyles Your Photos Using AI

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Photoshop Now Relights and Restyles Your Photos Using AI

Today at Adobe MAX 2026 in Miami Beach, Adobe announced a wide range of updates to its core applications and technologies, including Photoshop and Lightroom.

One of the key new features inside Adobe Photoshop is Relight. This is not necessarily a unique concept, as plenty of other apps and plugins have promised a similar concept: Fundamentally changing the “light source” in a photo with toggles and sliders.

Adobe says that Relight in Photoshop can dynamically add new light sources that naturally and non-destructively blend into the scene. Users can adjust the position, color, and brightness of their software-generated lights.

In a similar vein, the new Generative Restyle uses generative AI to turn text-based prompts into a new layer. Photographers can type how they want to style their image, and the AI does the rest. Well, mostly, as the intensity of the effect is still controlled manually via a slider.

Some familiar tools are getting major tweaks. For example, the Remove Tool is receiving a new “related elements” function. This automatically detects associated surrounding elements around an area the user paints over, including footprints, reflections, and shadows. It should be a time saver.

Likewise, a new Quick Compare makes it easier to see the before and after results for edits, rather than making users toggle between different states inside Photoshop.

AI Assisted Editor is not new, although it remains in beta inside Photoshop. The AI Assisted Editor is getting some big changes. There’s a new Camera Angles (beta) tool that lets users explore new viewpoints and perspectives inside an image. There’s also Generative Recompose that repositions and image into a new aspect ratio while promising to preserve the general composition. That could be useful for people trying to fit a landscape image to portrait orientation for social media. There’s also a tool to Convert to Text Layers, which extracts text from a flattened image and converts into an editable type layer.

Remove Ambient Effect Only sounds promising. It promises to remove distracting atmospheric effects, like lens flare, haze, or reflections, without removing any objects in a photo. If it works well, it could be a time saver.

Photoshop is also getting a new timelapse function that can turn the user’s entire creative editing process and workflow into a shareable timelapse video.

Over in Lightroom, there are many changes across the board.

Lightroom is getting in on the halation craze. A new Glow tool adds soft, cinematic light to photos. There’s a hazy Diffusion option or a “retro film-style” Halation tool. There are presets, although users can also fine-tune the effects to their preferences.

Blemish Removal, which was already in Lightroom on mobile, is now available in Lightroom on desktop. It includes new controls that Adobe says are suited to professional workflows and offers various fine-tuning options to ensure that only blemishes are removed, not natural elements like freckles.

Camera Tethering, a long-time area of dominance for Lightroom Classic compared to Lightroom, is getting a big update across Lightroom on Desktop and iPad. Adobe promises more ways to review, collaborate, and organize while shooting.

Finally, Open Eyes, arriving in public beta, can replace closed eyes with open ones. Adobe promises natural-looking results and the feature relies upon a reference image of the individual in the library, which Lightroom can find automatically. It’s not generating eyes from scratch.

While much of Adobe’s focus today is on Lightroom, rather than Lightroom Classic, Glow and Blemish removal are coming to Lightroom Classic as well, and the company promises some big news about other Lightroom Classic improvements and Adobe Camera Raw enhancements very soon, maybe even at MAX itself. There’s a lot going on in the Lightroom world right now.

Leica Unveils Cine Play 1 Plus 4K Projector With 4,200 Lumens

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Leica Unveils Cine Play 1 Plus 4K Projector With 4,200 Lumens

Leica has announced the Cine Play 1 Plus, an upgraded version of its compact 4K smart projector that increases brightness and contrast compared with its predecessor.

The Cine Play 1 Plus uses a Leica Summicron zoom lens alongside triple RGB laser technology and the company’s Leica Image Optimization (LIO) image processing system. It can project images measuring between 65 and 300 inches, with a maximum brightness of 4,200 ANSI lumens in Ultra mode.

Leica says the new model also offers an improved contrast ratio of 2,000:1, with the increased brightness designed to make projection more practical in rooms that aren’t completely dark.

The projector uses newly developed laser diodes and is designed for flexible use without permanent installation. Automatic image alignment and focus adjustment handle much of the setup process, allowing users to move the device between rooms or take it outside.

The Cine Play 1 Plus runs VIDAA 9.6, providing access to more than 400 pre-installed apps, including Netflix and Disney+, alongside free TV channels. It also supports voice control and features a dedicated Kids Zone.

Connectivity options include Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, Apple AirPlay, HDMI, USB, and LAN. The projector is also compatible with smart home platforms including Apple Home and Control4.

An integrated sound system supports Dolby Digital and DTS Virtual:X for spatial audio. Leica says the system is designed to complement the projector’s large-format image without requiring a separate sound system.

The Cine Play 1 Plus features an aluminium housing with a glass front and aluminium rear panel. An optional Leica Floor Stand 1 is also available, allowing the projector to be positioned around a room. The stand uses an integrated pin-contact system to provide power without visible cables.

Leica says the projector is built using durable materials and energy-efficient technology. It also comes with a protective carrying case made from expanded polypropylene (EPP), which is fully recyclable.

The Leica Cine Play 1 Plus is available now in the UK, Europe, and selected Asia-Pacific markets, including Australia, through Leica Stores and authorized TV and home cinema retailers.

The recommended retail price is £2,550 in the UK, and €2,995 in Europe.

Adobe Firefly is now a creative studio, and it means AI is everything everywhere all at once

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

How much control you keep is the big question as Adobe goes all in on AI ahead of Adobe Max.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Adobe has spent the past couple of years trying to convince us that generative AI belongs in the creative process, and at Adobe Max in Miami this week, it’s going all in. Here’s the thing: Firefly is no longer a text-to-image toybox for mum’s designing birthday invites. It’s being rebuilt as a proper, all-encompassing AI creative studio that pulls Adobe’s own tools, a stack of third-party AI models and a new Creative Agent into one place, and Adobe’s pitch is simple: if you bring the vision and judgement, its AI sidekick, the agent, will do the legwork.

Rather than prompting a model for one AI-made image, you describe what you want in plain language, and the new Agent orchestrates Adobe’s tools and a growing list of AI models to get there. You stay in the director’s chair, with the option to review the agent’s plan before it starts, pick a model yourself or leave it on auto-select, and step in whenever you like. Adobe believes this workflow will bring new projects within reach that were once out of reach and builds on Firefly and other Adobe AI tools added to Photoshop, Illustrator and Premiere Pro over the last three years.

It's a similar Agentic approach we learned about when Nvidia announced its plans for NVIDIA RTX Spark, to connect 3D apps in one AI-controlled space.

Firefly Design, in beta, is a new editor built from scratch, with vector, image editing, animation, 3D and print on one collaborative canvas. You can work with AI, use Adobe’s precision tools, or move between the two. The image editor gets new tools for objects, text and lighting, including Edit Object, Edit Text, Relight and Retouch, plus a gallery of dozens of Quick Actions for small jobs like removing an object or knocking out a thumbnail.

In a demo ahead of Adobe Max, I learned how it all links up. A Firefly Boards project, with fonts from Adobe Fonts’ library of around 35,000 typefaces, carried straight into the Firefly Video Editor. Adobe Stock supplied the music, ElevenLabs the text-to-speech, and the new cut detection did in seconds what Adobe’s own presenter says can take minutes, even hours. Title motion presets are in there too.

Firefly also offers models from Google, Kling, OpenAI, ByteDance and ElevenLabs alongside Adobe’s own and Topaz Labs’, with recent additions including Kling 4.0 and Dreamina Seedance 2.5. If you already pay for several of these services, seeing them under one roof will appeal. But this also raises more questions about Adobe’s famously ethical AI approach, as the foxes are now in the henhouse. I’ll be interested to see how this plays out.

Back to the details, Firefly Graph is in beta and lets you string tools and models together, save the result and run it again whenever you need to. Builder is still in private beta; you type a prompt, and it spits out a clickable prototype for a site or app, and Adobe claims it won’t wander off your design system. That’s the claim I’d test first, because most AI prototyping tools drift the moment you ask for something unusual.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Adobe is also previewing ‘always-on agents’ (sounds ominous) that keep recurring work moving while you’re away, like organising assets and checking quality. For example, a restaurant owner gives an agent their menus, URL, and brand assets. The agent drafts weekly social posts for approval, learns from feedback, and reports back on performance.

The agent is also spreading beyond Firefly into Adobe’s flagship desktop apps, including Illustrator, Premiere, InDesign, After Effects, and Frame.io, though the AI Assistants there are still in beta (in Photoshop only, in the Photoshop beta app). What caught my eye is that Adobe is also opening the door to rivals. The local MCP integrations, previewed now and due in private beta in early 2027, will let you point your preferred third-party agent at native Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere and After Effects project files on your own computer. Layers, vectors, bins and sequences stay editable, so you can step in and fix things yourself. Plenty of pros already use outside agents, and Adobe clearly wants its apps to do their work where they do it.

Away from the agent, the app updates keep coming. After Effects gets an AI assistant that can make sense of an inherited project, debug expressions and generate images and video. Photoshop gets Relight, Generative Restyle and Quick Compare, and its remove tool now spots associated elements such as shadows. Lightroom adds Glow, one-tap blemish removal and a beta Open Eyes fix for dodgy blinks. Separately, Topaz Labs, which Adobe recently acquired, has introduced two new models, Wonder 4 and Image HDR, for upscaling, denoising, deblurring, sharpening and HDR. Illustrator gets refreshed content libraries and new on-canvas Blend controls, and InDesign gets a co-editing beta, Flex Layout for text frames and rewrite tools.

The idea that the artist stays in charge is Adobe's way of easing bad news for any creative fearing how AI is affecting them, and I can believe that for filing, resizing, and checking. Whether that holds once the agent makes calls on composition, layout, and style is the big question, and the more capable these tools get, the blurrier that line will be.

Adobe still has to show that more automation really does mean more time spent making things, and that comes down to how much control creatives get once the demos are over. More importantly, with agentic AI now a trend and spreading to more and more creative software, artists and creatives who want to stay AI-free will find it harder than before.

Print on wood, metal, glass and more, with the best UV printers

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Explore projects, trends and inspiring design work.

Discover design software, tools and creative resources.

Start exploring exclusive deals, expert advice and more.

Packed with digital art tutorials, inspiration and expert advice from ImagineFX.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

These machines offer new creative opportunities to designers, illustrators and crafters.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Until recently, if you wanted to print onto, say, a phone case, a slate coaster or a leather notebook cover, you'd have to either pay a print shop, or blow a fortune on an industrial flatbed. Now, magically, that's all changed.

A new wave of desktop UV printers has arrived at affordable prices and manageable sizes. And they offer exciting new creative opportunities to designers, illustrators, crafters and anyone wanting to make stuff to sell on Etsy.

A UV printer jets ink onto an object and cures it instantly with ultraviolet light, so the ink sits on top of the surface rather than soaking in. If you want, you can build it up in layers to create raised, tactile textures. Many of the best UV printers also add white ink for printing onto dark or clear materials, and a varnish channel for gloss and spot effects. Of course, there's also the old fashioned choice of the best 3D printers out there, but make no mistake – UV printers use use a far more sophisticated (and expensive) technology.

In the article below, I've listed the three best UV printers on the market today, along with two Kickstarter projects that are well worth keeping an eye on (and potentially supporting, if you believe in the project).

xTool is best known for making some of the best laser cutters, and the O1 Omni is its first UV printer, which it describes as a 4-in-1.

You can use it for UV printing onto standard surfaces. It can also make UV DTF transfer stickers for curved and awkward surfaces. And the top-of-the-range, "UV + DT" model can print directly onto cotton (DTG), and onto film for heat transfer to fabric (DTF). If you want to do all of those things, then, you're saving yourself from buying two or three separate machines.

It's also very precise. Rather than relying on a fixed overhead camera, which distorts things towards the edge of the frame, xTool's Pixel-Scan system combines laser sensing with a scanning imager to map the bed one-to-one.

On the downside, the O1 Omni prints a whole job at the height of the tallest object, so mixing a chunky item with flat coasters can leave the lower ones blurred. It's also big and heavy (41kg) so you'll need a sturdy surface to work from. On the plus side, xTool's cartridges are pretty affordable. And overall, if you want one machine that does nearly everything, this is the top dog.

Eufymake is a sub-brand of global hardware company Anker, and the E1 is arguably the machine that kicked off the desktop UV boom. It became the most-funded project in Kickstarter history, made TIME's list of Best Inventions of 2025, and is now on sale generally.

If you want something that's easy to set up and use, this is it. The UV printer arrives fully assembled, the cartridge system means you don't have to mess about with syringes or bottles, and it's quick and easy to get from unboxing to a first print. The E1 stacks ink up to 5mm high, allowing 3D texture effects. You can in theory, then, reproduce brushstrokes on a canvas print, raised lettering on a business card or a leathery grain on a phone case. There's an optional rotary attachment for printing on curved surfaces like mugs and tumblers.

It's not perfect: the cartridges are more expensive than rivals', so if you're printing at scale that's worth considering. On the plus side, it's around half the size and weight of the Xtool, making it easy to fit into a home studio. In short, this is the one we'd recommend if you're new to the world of UV printing.

Procolored has been making DTF and UV printers for small print businesses for years. Its UV printer, the Procolored V4, was created primarily for one specific job: printing onto phone cases.

The 120 x 170mm A5 bed with a 15mm height limit is the perfect size for a large smartphone case laid flat. An infrared sensor measures the height of what's on the bed, and you align your designs using printed guides. The V4 is able to print onto cases made of hard plastic, soft silicone and glass. It doesn't just work on phones, either: Other small, flat items such as acrylic keyrings, badges, tiles and metal nameplates work too, as long as they fit within the print area.

Ink comes from bottles not cartridges, which helps keep running costs affordable. The V4 automatically cleans its head every 10 hours when it's left on, using about 1ml of ink a day, to keep the nozzles from clogging.

On the downside, it's slower than the newer machines and there's no varnish channel for gloss or raised textures. The software only works on Windows, and isn't the simplest to use. And for only slightly more money, you can get one of the larger all-rounders above. If you only want to print on phone cases, though, the V4 delivers exactly what it promises.

These UV printers haven't actually shipped yet, so we haven't included them in our main list. They're close to doing so though, so assuming all goes well, they're well worth keeping an eye on.

Most desktop UV printers slide the bed back and forth under the printhead. On the Morpho UV Printer, the head moves on a gantry, allowing more space for taller objects: 168mm of clearance, to be specific. The bed will bear items up to 10kg, and Morpho is also promising G7-certified colour calibration for print-shop consistency. It looked pretty good to us at the consumer electronics show IFA this September: here's a short video of it making a bookmark for us.

We don't know much more at this stage, though. This is a crowdfunded product, with shipping estimated between December 2026 and January 2027. But hands-on reports have been positive, and if tall or heavy objects are your thing, it's worth watching closely.

Heygears has spent years making resin 3D printers for dental and audio manufacturing, and with the G1X, it's applying that expertise to UV printing. In its standard form it's a high-end desktop UV printer, but it's built to grow: the Full-3D Pack will add a resin system that lets the same machine build full-colour 3D objects layer by layer.

Even if you never touch the 3D side, though, it looks like a serious UV printer. It uses Epson's industrial i3200 head, which is rated for up to two years and is less prone to clogging, along with eight ink channels for a wider colour range. Again, this is a crowdfunded launch, with shipping targeted for December.

In general, the best UV printers can print on any rigid, flat surface: wood, acrylic, metal, glass, slate, leather, ceramic, plastic and canvas, along with ready-made blanks like phone cases, coasters, keyrings and notebook covers. However, there are exceptions. To print onto clothing, you'll need a machine with DTG or DTF capability, such as the Xtool O1 Omni UV + DT edition. Very smooth surfaces such as glass and metal may need a primer for the best adhesion. And you'll need a rotary attachment for mugs, tumblers and bottles.

Generally yes, as long as you use common sense. However, uncured UV ink can irritate skin, so wear gloves when changing ink or cleaning your printer, and keep it away from children and pets. The inks can also give off a smell, so use the printer in a well-ventilated room.

Ink is the main ongoing cost of running a UV printer, and prices vary widely between brands. So compare the cost per millilitre and check whether the printer uses its own cartridges or refillable bottles. The printhead is a consumable too: cheaper heads may need replacing every six to 12 months. It's worth noting that UV printers need regular use, as ink that's left sitting around for too long can clog the head.

ZWO Seestar S30 Plus review

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

We tested the brand-new ZWO Seestar S30 Plus, an all-in-one smart telescope for beginners and hobbyists that makes astrophotography accessible to everyone.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

There's much to enjoy about the Seestar S30 Plus. The image quality is great, it's easy to use, and it's compact and lightweight enough to take it anywhere. It's not quite as good as the more expensive S30 Pro, but it's a nice middle-ground option between the S30 and the S30 Pro that gives you a lot of features for the money.

Seestar app is reliable and easy to navigate

All-in-one workflow from shooting to sharing

2K image quality is better than we expected

Editing controls could be a little more responsive

Not suitable for smaller planetary nebulas

Why you can trust Live Science Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best ones for you. Find out more about how we test.

ZWO has just released the newest member of its smart telescope range: the Seestar S30 Plus. This all-in-one smart telescope is designed for beginners and hobbyists who want an affordable way into astrophotography that won't leave you scratching your head, and lets you capture, process and share your images in one simple workflow. It sits between the Seestar S30 and S30 Pro, and is priced at $499 for its release.

With 2K image quality, a light pollution filter and a user-friendly interface, it's a smart telescope that anyone can enjoy, regardless of your experience level, and regardless of whether you're shooting from your city balcony or a dark sky site. Like the other models, it uses dual cameras to capture a wide range of subjects, including deep-sky objects with the telephoto camera, and constellations, star trails and the Milky Way with the wide-angle camera. It's lightweight and easy to travel with, and it even comes with a separate solar filter for solar imaging and eclipse time-lapses.

We got our hands on an advance copy to test ahead of its release, so we put it through its paces on several different celestial subjects.

The Seestar S30 Plus is one of the smallest, most compact smart telescopes on the market, weighing just 3.63 pounds (1.65 kg) and measuring 8.3 x 5.5 x 3.2 inches (210 x 140 x 80 mm). Its sleek, matte white design looks identical to the other Seestar models (we compared it to our S30 Pro), except for the branding on the unit itself.

It comes in a padded soft carry case and includes a small tripod (although we always prefer to use our own), a solar filter, a 40-inch (1 meter) USB-A to USB-C charging cable, a removable shoulder strap for the carry case, and a quick-start guide. With its accessories and case, the whole setup weighs 4.89 pounds (2.22 kg).

On the unit itself, you'll find just one button, which powers it on, plus the USB-C port on the side. Super simple, although we'd have liked the USB-C port to be covered to prevent moisture or dust from getting in.

It comes with a carry case, strap, USB-C cable and solar filter.

The set-up process couldn't be easier. When you turn the telescope on, you'll hear a double beep and see the power button light up alongside the red dots on the black screen to indicate the battery level. Four dots mean the battery is full. The telescope will tell you when it's ready to connect, at which point you can sync it up to the app on your smartphone or tablet. To turn it off, you just select "Shutdown" in the app, which closes the arm and turns the unit off.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

It also features an anti-dew heater, which we've found works quite well in mild to moderate humidity. On a couple of our shoots, we noticed a tiny bit of condensation forming on the unit when the humidity was higher, so we wouldn't feel confident leaving it unsupervised for long periods on a particularly dewy evening. (We used the Clear Outside app to monitor the humidity levels.)

The Seestar App is user-friendly and easy to navigate, with just the right amount of controls: you have plenty of options to choose from but it doesn’t become overwhelming. The main Seestar page is where you'll likely spend most of your time, as this is where you can search for celestial objects to shoot, access your album, plan a shoot and read up on what objects Seestar recommends based on your time and location.

The Stargazing section is where you can select or search for the subject you want to shoot. You can use the search function to find a specific target or browse Tonight's Best, and you can also search through different types of subjects like galaxies, nebulas, clusters and more. From here, you can also select planets, track comets and photograph constellations using the wide camera.

Click on a subject and the app will display information about it, including its current altitude and coordinates, which help determine what time is best to shoot that particular object. It automatically applies the appropriate filters and settings, but you can override these if you want to.

There's very little to complain about when it comes to using the app. It has lots of information for beginners, so you don't need a ton of knowledge about the night sky, and everything is fairly self-explanatory. There were a couple of occasions when it failed to calibrate itself (for example, when we tried to locate the Pleiades, which had just risen above a tree), and other times when we chose a new target and it slewed the long way round instead of only having to move slightly. But otherwise, the app worked well.

The AI Enhance section has some good editing tools to bring out the best from your photos. You can reduce the stars, adjust the brightness, contrast, saturation and RGB curves, apply denoise and eliminate any satellite trails — although we'd love a dehaze slider to bring out some of the fainter nebulosity of certain objects. The sliders could do with being a little more responsive, especially the RGB curves, but overall, it does a pretty good job of editing your pictures to get a pleasing result.

We had about 10 days to test our review unit before its release, so we could only capture as much as the weather permitted in that time. We'll add more images to this review over the coming weeks. All our tests were conducted from our backyard in a Bortle 4 region, and captured before moonrise to eliminate as much light pollution as possible. Due to these time constraints, we aimed to capture our images for a total of 60 minutes each, although we'd have ideally aimed for longer in some cases.

Now for the fun part — the images. The Seestar S30 Plus has a focal length of 160mm, which is advantageous for capturing large subjects like the Andromeda Galaxy, the North America Nebula and the Pleiades. Large emission nebulas are where it really shines; however, the focal length is too short to capture smaller apparent objects.

For this review, we aimed to capture as many different types of objects as possible. All our images were edited in the Seestar app with, in some cases, a few minor tweaks in Lightroom. You also have the option to download the individual files to your computer to process them yourself in dedicated astrophotography software, which is advantageous if you're a more experienced user.

Everyone's favorite star cluster, the Pleiades, was one of the first targets we wanted to capture. We could see the faint blue nebulosity after as little as 15 minutes of integration time, and it only improved over time. After 60 minutes, we had a really nice image and could easily bring out the blue haze via the editing tools in the app. However, we got a much more impressive result after tweaking our image a little further in Lightroom, bringing out even more haze and reflective dust.

The Andromeda Galaxy is a perfect target for the Seestar S30 Plus. It takes up a large portion of the frame, and with long integration times, it can reveal beautiful details in the faint outer arms. We shot for around an hour in our tests due to weather and time constraints, but we still got an impressive image. As we approached 60 minutes, there was a lot of cloud cover, and we noticed it struggling to stack images toward the end of the session, so we stopped the integration at 57 minutes.

Andromeda is certainly going to be the most impressive galaxy for the Seestar visually, as most others will appear smaller in the frame and will need longer integration, so make sure you have a clear, dark night for it.

Large emission nebulas are where the Seestar S30 Plus thrives, and the North America Nebula is a great target. It covers the whole field of view, and the red hydrogen gas easily comes to the forefront of the image.

The Eastern Veil Nebula is another lovely target where you can bring out the beautiful red and turquoise hues in as little as 15 minutes, but longer integration times will yield brighter, more colorful results.

Planetary nebulas are where you will see the limitations of the Seestar's 160mm focal length. Most planetary nebulas have a very small apparent size — we've previously captured the Dumbbell Nebula and Cocoon Nebula with our S30 Pro, and they were a tiny dot in the middle of the screen. We tried the Helix Nebula, and even though it's one of the largest apparent planetary nebulas, it's still fairly small on the Seestar S30 Plus. You can still get a decent image of it, but if you want to focus on imaging planetary nebulas, you'll need a bigger scope. Because it was very low in the sky and there was some interfering cloud, we stopped integration at 27 minutes.

The Seestar S30 Plus is an affordable entry point into deep-sky astrophotography without the confusing settings and expensive setup. It's ideal for beginners, although more experienced users will likely want to upgrade to the superior image quality and extra features of the S30 Pro.

Still, for under $500, we can't complain. Not only is the Seestar S30 Plus easy to navigate and use, but it teaches you about space in a fun and interactive way, and you don't need to spend hours stacking individual frames on your computer to process — although you still can do that if you want more control over your editing.

If you can stretch your budget, the S30 Pro is a worthy upgrade. It produces 4K images and offers more pro-level features.

To see deeper into space, the Seestar S50 Pro has a longer focal length of 260mm and a larger 50mm aperture, making it better for smaller apparent objects.

If the imaging aspect doesn't interest you, the Celestron StarSense Explorer range of telescopes connects to your phone to guide you around the sky, but you observe the cosmos through an eyepiece instead of capturing it on your phone.

Read our full Celestron StarSense Explorer LT 70AZ review

Kimberley Lane, E-commerce writer for Live Science, has tested a wide range of optical equipment, reviewing camera gear from Sony, Canon, OM System and more. With over 6 years of photography experience, her skills span across landscape and seascape photography, wildlife, astrophotography and portrait work. Her photos have been featured in a number of national magazines, including Digital Camera World and Cosmopolitan. She has also contributed to our sister site Space.com and Tech Radar, and she regularly uses binoculars and telescopes to stargaze in the dark skies of South Wales.
